TEE SOIITIT CIIESHIRE iilUNT.—The South Cheshire hounds found an excellent lot of foxes after meeting at Burleaton Smithy on Tuesday. They first tried con- clusions with a Wardle fox, which after considerable bugeting went to ground in an earth which bad bean frequented in force by badgers. A strong litter of cubs at the Bache Reuse covers drew upon themselves a fierce attack, which led the complete discomfiture of all but one member of the family, and this fox, draw— ing upoa himself the undivided attentions of the pack, was finaly rolled over. A copious downfall of rain has greatly improved the country.

===== 0002601_19011011_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
LADIES' LETTER.

Aix Dian NELLIE, Soft drap de fentre picture hats, with the brims laced through slits at intervals with soft silk or velvet are likely to take on, for they are eminently becoming to English faces. Very pretty they look in cream felt, or in pale blue or mauve, laced with black velvet, and trimmed with large black ostrich feathers, with a soft spreading bow of the velvet behind. The alternative in hats seems to be the double plateau, two brims of soft felt or velvet divided by twists of ribbon, chrysanthe- mums, or birds or ostrich feathers. The tricorne and the large Empire and cavalier shapes will be most worn. There is no likelihood of the hair being worn high for outdoors, as the flat-crowned hats render such a style quite impossible ; there seems also a tendency to dress it in flat bandeaux on either side of the face in front. Tricorne hats have their brims edged with a fluffy silk male, and a large cockade to match in front of the same.

Appearnces seem to show that the inevitable reaction has set in with regard to the hitherto fashionable loose-backed coats. So ugly are they, and so diagarin, when 'badly made or ungrace- fully worn, that it is surprising that they have not long before now been banished from the ward- robes of the smartly dressed. Now, however, the newest coat is tight-fitting, the basque being rather long and cut away from the front like the skirts of a man's morniog coat. The revers are faced with long lines of braid sewn -closely together on black silk, and the sleeves are finished with deep cuffs arranged After a similar fashion. The military style in autumn and winter coats never goes out of fashion, and in this -instance the braid disposed in loops across the white cloth plastron in Iront has quite a military air. The skirt of the gown is quite plain, except for a few rows of stitchings down the front seam. That cloth and serge gowns will have plain skirts is a eireuni• stance that means god all round. The workers are tired of over- ela'ooration as displayed on summer dresses, and the eye is weary of the frettings and the frillings and the fkiuneings. It is a real rest to worker and to the gensral eye when this folds fall sheer and unbros.pa in line.

The thrifty always send their furs to be re. modelled before the furriers become busy, but not ere they know what type of pattern will be smart and fashionable for the winter. Now is a happy moment for the overhauling of such treasures. Old-world tippets are going to form the basis upon which new furs are to be planned with long straight stole ends' quaintly drawn together by means of a fur bow. Naturally the bow is rather flat, but none the less old-world looking for that. Now and then fur shoulder wraps are acceptable already, though muffs seem a long way from their period of success. From the newest ideas in furs it is evident that there is a great tendency towards pelerines and ruffles rather than towards very heavy garments in fur. The first is a tippet in mink or sable, the yoke bordered with a broad cross-band of the fur, and ornamented here and there with clusters of tails holding the stole ends in at the waist. The large muff matches. A tippet is very pretty when composed of two capes fastened in front with a flat b)w of fur and loug ends. The collar and revers are cut in one and rolled back from the face.

White will be all the rage with those who can afford it and it is stated that white serge and zebelline, with white and brown fur trimmings for outdoor wear, and for the evening white soft satin, brocade, broche, and the shimmering cloud- like chiffons and crepes are to be very fasloonable. White has prevailed so universally for months past, and has proved so exquisitely becoming that it is unlikely to lose its popularity at present. Black is its only rival. For those who prefer colours, there is a reciprocity shown between the season and the choice available; the golden browns of October, the russet reds and the lovely very mellow deep peens are at their disposal.

Flowers are now worn in the hair again. They require careful treatment. If the face is short, and the wearer not too tall, the flowers can be worn high in the hair, nearly over the forehead. If the wearer is tall, with regular features, the flowers may nestle under the left ear. Dark women of a classical style of beauty may we.tr a wres.th of foliage, or pearls braided in their dusky tresses. The blonde looks best with a bow of ribbon, either of black velvet or some becoming shade, an osprey, or something light and dainty. A jewelled tulle butterfly is also very becoming. Your loving JENNIE.

WOMEN'S OHM.

The Duke of Norfolk, as Earl Marshal, has a very laborious task in order to master all the ins and outs of the Coronation ceremony. It is the one great historic Court pageantoa bleb the precent generation of courtiers has not seen, and the time, from now to next June, is not a whit too long for the necessary preparations arid rehearsals. On some former occasions considerable confusion has existed in celebrations of the rite. George _ _ .

HI. complaioed of the arrangements for his Coronatio-n, and Lord Effingham replied that the Earl Marshal's Office bad been strangely neglected, but that he bad taken care for the future that the nezt Coronation would be regulated in regret order. The King was so amused at this speech, that be made the Earl repeat his astnnishing gaucherie several times. The Duke of Norf..lk, however, is taking time by the forelock, and all the Peers and ' Peeresses who are entitled to a Royal Command to attend the Coronation, must send in their applications by the end of the year. The Duke has also issued an Order concerning the robes and coronets to be worn, and these instructions are redolent of the fine distinctions which exist within the aristocracy. All the robes are to be of crimson velvet, but the rows of ermine or fur trimmings are to be according to degree of nobility :—A Baron will have two rows ; a Viscount two rows and a half ; an Earl three rows ; and a Duke four rows. It is the same with the Coronets, which are to be of silver gilt without jewels. That of a Baron will have six silver balls, while a Viscount will have sixteen. A Marquis will have four silver balls, and four gold strawberry !saves, and ' a Duke will have eight gold strawberry leaves. Such are the fine gradations of rank, and in the case of the Peeresses they are carried to still more exquisite preciseness. In addition to similar distinctions as relate to the Peers, a Baroness is allowed a train of cnly one yard, a Viscountess, one of a yard and a quarter ; a Countess, a yard and a half ; a Marchioness, a yard and three quarters ; and a Duchens two yards. Ore tremb:es to think what would happen if a Countess appeared in the full length train ot a Duchess. The medieval air about all these arrangements almost suggests that the unfortunate lady would be hurried off and executed according to ancient custom, at the historic Tower of London.

These widows of Peers who have re-married with a commoner are precluded by the Earl Marshals Order, from being present at the Coronation. Among others who are affected by this order,are the Dowager Duchess of Sutherland, who is married to Sir Albert Rollit, the Dowager Marchioness of Tweeddale, who is the wife of Major Evans Gordon, and the Countess of Portarlington whose second husband is below the rank of the peerage. Peeresses in their own right will retain their full privi- -1 gee, and the Baroness Burdett Coutts, though married to a commoner,will be entitled to attend the Coronation, and she is the doyenne of the peeresses. It is not:long since Lord Rosetery had to contradict a matrimonial report about himself, and there is no truth in the unauthorised announcement of the engagement of Lady Sybil Primrose, who since Lady Crew's marri- age has alone undertaken for her father all the duties of a hostess in a leading social position. Lady Sybil, it will be remembered, had the rare honour of a private presentation to Queen Victoria, upon her entrance Into society some two or three years ago. King Edward was present at her debut dance, and Lady Sybil has also acted as hostess at Dalmeny, to the Duke and Duchess of Cornwall and York.

There is Mlle idea that Lord Rosebery will acquire the historic Battle Abbey, lately the residence of his mother, the Duchess of Cleveland, and if so Lady Sybil Primrose will be called upon to reign over yet another of Lord Rosebery'a beautiful heroes. The estate Is definitely announced for sale:on the 28th November and if it is not to be secured for the nation it is highly desirable that it shculd pass into the hands of some nobleman, who like the late Duchess of Cleveland, will I carefully preserve its historical associations. _ It seems like going back to the time of our grand mothers to return to the heavy pendant earrings with which they used to weight and disfigure their ears. Yet it seems they are " coming in "—though it may be doubted whether such a fashion will ever again become popular. Our parents with not half the freedom or activity which now distinguishell the feminine world, found them inconvenient, and gradually abandoned the heavy pendants for the simple ring or stud ornament. Even these have more or less disappeared of late years, and we could well afford to let ear piercing remain the almost forgotten art it has become. Given a few yards of suitable lace, and a furlong or so of velvet baby-ribbon, and there are few things the clever modiste cannot evolve. Charming blouses and fronts are being built of these two items, while many of the smart toques, too, in evidence just now, are of piece lace tbreadcd thrcugh and through with black velvet ribbon, the Whole thrown together with inimitable skill. No other trimmings appear, neither LlowerS, wings, or feathers, just the shape draped with the lace, so arranged as to form a light Cascade at the back,over which fall loops and ends of the velvet, left free in this °op position only. Needless to remark, only skilled modiste3 could build such a form of headgear. Buttons are Well to the fore. Little gilt specimens ie twos and threes anpear upon tabs, pechets, revers, aed cuffs. Larger ones embellish cloth and fur:coats, or are studded on box-pleats, or down the front of travelling cloaks. Buttons also appear en waist bands, not as fastenings, but as ornaments, end are sometimes so lavishly used, as to almost hide the foundation to which they are sewn. In tbis position it is not an entire set that encircles the waist but a collection of " odds," more or less of a site, and—presumably—each with a history attached to it. The arrangement sug- gests/a:row of medals, warlike or otherwise, and to collect sufficient for the parpose,is at least as interestiag as was the gathering together of beads for chains. Up-to-date chops are showing dainty silk stocks with high stiff collars and long boss in front. These suggest , an excellent wa) of brightening up a dull dress, and ef utiliaing stray remnants. They are not difficult ti make, hooks and eyes forming the real fastenings at the back of the collar, bright pins being run through the bows in front. Cosy little finiehes are these stocks, worn under coats open at the neck, and moreover they form a dressy covering for the only scrap of dress bodice, that would otherwise be exposed. Sets, consisting of toque, muff, and nrcttlet, built of peacock's feathers,are appearing and despite the super- stition attached to the plumage of this bird, are being donned by the emartest of smart women. This par- ticular craze is not likely to be long indulged in by the exclusive ones, as imitation eets promise to find all too great favor with the masses, a very little later on. The peacock, the proverbial emblem of vanity is well-known to he a long-lived bird, but there is one it seems at Warwick Castle which is over a hundred years old. No one, of course, can remember it in its early youth, but its history can be traced through successive generations of servants, and according to an article on "Some of Lady Warwick's Pets " in Country Life, there would seem to be no doubt as to the great age of this particular bird. There is another peacock at the Castle which was given to the late Earl of Warwick by Lord Beaconsfield, sbout 30 years ago. The Earl of Beacons- field was very fond of peacocks, and there are Several in different parts of the country, which came from Hugh- enden Manor, his place in Buckinghamshire. ME= EZEKti MADGE.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, OCTOBER 11 1901

A

(--CHAPTER L' TROOPER BEN. A sturdy young man rose upright in a hay. field, ciaightened hi 's aching back, and drew. a sunburnt arm across his dripping brow. He whetted his scythe with the rubstone, and then placed it on the ground while he drank a long, deep draught -from -a jug of home-brewed ale. "Ali," he murmured, "that's good. It'll keep me on till I've done this patch and I shall be just in time for drill, after all lie took up his seethe, and with steady sweeps of the long curved blade cut down the graceful grals before him. When.he rose to stretch him- self again the mowing of the field was done. He shouldered his scythe, took his coat from the wall near him, and left 'the field as the other hay- makers, who had been to the farmhouse for tea, came back to spread the grass. Mark Benn, with his coat on his shoulder and the scythe resting on the garment, walked slowly ftlong the path which led to Hill Top , Farm his ,father's house, and the dwelling-place of Benny for generations. Ile-re the staunch old farmers had lived and died, beginning with the Nark who built the place, and who, lying sick in the parlour and knowing that his time had come, ordered a mare and a young foal to be brought to his bedside, and having said farewell to the mother, end generously admired ,the spindle- leg4ed son, fell back and passed away in peace. The sun was low in the sky, but the beams were strong and warm behind the mower as he walked, and they fell full on the back of the farmhouse and the outbuildings. Mark looked on the structures almost with an owner's prille. They were not yet his, and would not, he hoped, belong to any man but his father for many years. The ad farmer—he had reached hi s seventieth birthday— was bard and enduring, and it seemed as if he would do as more than one of his ancestors had done, and get well into the 'eighties before he began to say that he had lived quite long enough and wished to go; for it Was a point of honour with the race to do their passing to the churchyard on the hill two miles away' as comfortably as they had lived upon the farm. To Mark there came through the drowsy air the smell of the new-mown hay which men were stacking in the great barn that stood on one side of the farmyard behind the house; the sound of their voices as they worked, the creaking of the carts as the forks were dug deep down and the lay was lifted to the workers on the stacks ; the lowing of the cattle .in the mistal, the contented grunts of pigs, and the noisy cackle of some wandering hens. The sun was fiery on the windows and hot on the roofs of the buildings, and the young man felt that he would like to throw himself upon the nearest haycock and sleep. Since four o'clock in the morning he had been at work with his scythe among the yielding grass. It was now seven, and he had not taken much more than an hour's rest for meals in the whole of the fifteen. He had cut broad pathways in the grass, going forward steadily and keeping to his task even when the other workers had eft the field and gone to the farm to tea; so that when they returned he could leave them and get away for the night to put in his drill as a Yeomanry cavalryman. At the end of the path on which he walked there was a gate that opened into the farmyard itself. Through this he passed, and put his scythe into, a shed. Then he entered the open door of the dairy, where bowls of cool milk were standing, 'and by another door went into the family living-room itself. This was a great, low room, with a sanded floor, and a mitered ceiling from which hams and sides of bacon hung. There was a long, low window, mullioned and with diamond panes, a big door, now wide open, through wilich, across a gravelled space, there could be seen a garden thick with gooseberry and currant bushes, brought up at the bottom with a clump of apple trees. As Mark entered the room a quiet woman, capped, white-aproned, took a steaming kettle from the fire and brewed some tea. With a great sigh of satisfaction the young man drew an arm- chair up to the quaint round table on which his evening meal was laid, and, just as he had come froM the hayfield, except that he opened the front of his shirt to cool himself the better, ate and drank until he had had enough. He poured out a last cup of tea, using his saucer as a drinking vessel. With another sigh, this .time of content- ment, he rose and strode to the door. "Are you goin' back to the Long Meadow?" asked the quiet woman. "No, it's finished. I've been wiring into it," answered the young man. "It's been a tough job, but I swore I'd do it so's I could get off to my drill." "Father wants you to go he'd an' give a hand at spreadin'," said the woman, hesitatingly. "He wouldn't if his bones and back-ached u much as mine do," answered Mark, with a growl. This was unpleasant news for him. He was resolute in his determination to attend the drill, and yet, twenty-five though he was in. years he had never openly rebelled against his father's wishes, the wishes which. were just as good as orders. "It's only because he's so anxious, an' because the bay's so good this time, the best there's been for years," said Mrs. Penn, anxiously. "He's afraid it'll rain soon, an wants to get it all in before then," The young man stood for some time looking at the sanded floor. Then, without speaking, he stepped on to'the flags which ran in front of the house and emptied a • pailful of rainwater into a wooden bowl on a stone table under the window. With a piece of brown soap he washed and splashed himself in the open air with sundry noises, and rubbed himself into a glorious glow with a rough towel. The water he poured down a neighbouring sink, and having rinsed the bowl and put it upon edge to dry—for Mark was a methodical and tidy man—he strode strongly across the floor, crunching the sand, and went upstairs to his bed- room. liere he got into his Yeoman's uniform, and when he was completely dressed, descended and walked out on to the gravel in front of the house. The sturdy and somewhat clumsy young man with the seythe stood there transformed, a clean-limbed, active trooper, whose hard, courageous face looked more severe and daring under the metal helmet, and whose broad, strong back appeared wider and more muscular in the scarlet tunic. Mark had put the farmer's air aside, and with his regimentals had assumed a perfect military carriage and demeanour. Seeing him there you might have taken him to be a trained dragoon. As the trooper went towards the stable he met his father, "Where are you going?" asked the slid man, bluntly. "To drill," replied Mark, as bluntly. "I want the Long Meadow finishin', continued the farmer. "I 'xpected you to go back an' give a hand in the spreadin' when you'd done your tea." "I can't go back to-night," said the young man. "There's a full dress parade, and I-promised the Captain faithfully that I'd turn up." "And who gave you leave?" asked the farmer, with tightening mouth. "I didn't think I need ask for it," answered Mark, whose sunburnt face was deepening in colour. "Surely at my age I needn't come an' ask about a thing like that?" "Your grandfather would ha' felled me for less talk than that," said the old man, grimly. "That may be," retorted Mark, "but he wouldn't have felled me." He looked defiant as he spoke, and clenched his fists instinctively. "Well," said the farmer, quietly, "we needn't waste time in talkin'. Go an' get them things off." The trooper remained standing and, silent. A fierce struggle was going on within him—a straggle for emancipation. So far throughout his life he had given unquestioning obedience to his father. It had been the Benne' way down the ages. "Come," added the farmer, moving slowly off. "I was going to saddle up Prince," answered Mark, speaking in hard tones. "Prince is wanted for leadin'," replied the fanner. For a moment the trooper was in doubt as to what he should do. The headquarters were five miles away, the parade was a mounted one, and it would be useless to go without his horse. He WAS proud of Prince, too, for he was one of the smartest and most useful mounts in the regiment, where the horseflesh was varied, and in many Cases doubtful. "Then I'll go without," said Mark. "Anyway, I will if Jackson can't lend me his mare." "Oh!" said the farmer, stopping and MAI hison, "so you're bent on goin' 1! ._ --

atn," relilied the son, half-pleadingly. "I've been in the Long Meadow fifteen hours to-day, working like a fiend, sb thali I could get off to- night. Besides, I promised the Captain. "And the Captain, I suppose, is more nor the Long Meadow—or me?" said the farmer, with a sneer. "He's a very nice fellow, and I'm not going to break my word with him," said Mark. "Well, so and good," replied the farmer, moving off again. "You're old enough to know your own mind. I'm not goin' to drag You back; but if I wanted to go in for soldierin' I'd go in for the real thing. I woukln't be a noodle." With a bitter sneer he turned away and went to the Long Meadow. By the time he had picked up a fork and was furiously scattering the grass which Mark had cut he was full of sorrow for his hasty words; but his stubborn pride prevented him from doing as he longed to do, go back, tell Mark to saddle Prince and get away and enjoy himself at the parade. "After, all," he muttered, "the lad's done more work to-day than any two men in the fields. It's too late for me to mend, or I 'might curb my tongue a bit. shall do mischief with it some day. _

'CHAPTER IL • - EOM WA* TO REALITY.

Stung by his father's sneer at this sort •of soldiering—it was not the first time the old man had openly expressed contempt for the Yeomanry —Mark flushed deeper than before. He was almost ashamed of his warlike aspect, because he had done so little to support it, and afraid to move lest his sword and spurs should clank; but that feeling left him, and he turned with firm steps towards the farm of which he had spoken, the dwelling-place of 'Jackson. This homestead was half a mile away, and by the time he had reached it his bitterness had passed. The grim face had softened, and the young man's bright blue eyes glistened with anticipation. Jackson was seated in a chair under his parlour window, dozing. He did not see the trooper, but the flash of the sun on the helmet had been seen by Jackson's daughter, and she was looking proudly through a little diamond pane. at the afproaching figure. She had been watching for him this half-hour, but had not expected him to come like this. It was as a mounted soldier that she had waited for Mark Bean, and she wondered what the meaning of his walking was. Why was he not riding up on Prince? The trooper clanked up to the unconscious farmer, but the figure did not move. With a smile Pe walked into the house, where the daughter, who feigned deep interest in a newspaper, sprang to her feet, and asked how Mark had got there, and why he startled her in this way? "And where's Prince ? " she demanded.

"Leading hay," replied Mark, briefly. "Father wouldn't let me have him. He seems to think I'm in petticoats yet. So I've come to borrow the mare. Can I have her?"

"Of course," answered the girl. "Come on. She's in fine condition. She's been idling all day. It's time she worked."

"But hadn't we better ask your father, Liz ? " "Oh, no. It'll be time enough when he wakes. Let him go on dozing. What a beautiful uniform. I'm sure anybody would take you for a real soldier, Mark, instead of only a Yeoman." Lizzie spoke in honest admiration, but her inno- cent words stung Mark afresh. They impressed upon him the truth as his father's taunt had done —that people did not take him seriously, and that he only drilled, shot, rode, and wore a uniform to swagger a bit, as neighbours put it. Some had gone a little further, and had admitted that the Yeomanry were at least useful for something—they were good enough to be shot at.

LI7.ZiO led the way towards the stable and took down the saddle for the mare. When the two had made her ready Mark led the animal forth b,nd mounted. He was a first-rate rider, and looked even more soldierly in the saddle than he did on foot. The admiring girl noticed this and com- mented on it. "I tell you," she said, "that you look just as real as those men did that rode through here last autumn on their way to York dragoons they were called. Most impudent they were. One of them asked me for a drink of water, and squeezed my hand so when I brought it that I threw it in his face."

"I should have pulled him out of the saddle if I'd been near, dragoon or no dragoon," said Mark. "I'll have no interference with a girl o' mine." "Well, don't get angry, Mark,, " replied the girl, "or I shan't tell you these little bits of things. You ought to laugh at them, as I do. You've no sense of humour. You're like the parson." "I hope not," answered Mark. "But as to humour, I don't see some things in the same light as you, perhaps. But there, I'm a bit upset. You all seem to think I'm a fraud. You'd be a bit surprised if I took it into nay head to become Übe real thing, wouldn't you?" "Don't talk nonsense," said Lizzie, uneasily. "I don't like to hear it. People often say things without meaning them." "And often mean a good deal more than they say, eh, Liz? But there, I shall be late. I've half-an-hour to get to the parade in, and it's a:- five mile trot on a bad road. So long." He smiled and rode away. Lizzie watched him until he gained the high road, then entered the house and ran upstairs, and from her bedroom window kept him in sight while the sun glittered on the helmet. Again she was possessed with a strong feeling of pride at seeing her lover in such a handsome uniform and shelving it off so well. Her pride was all the greater because it was unmixed with fear, the fear that Mark would ever be called upon to do more than wear his regimentals as he was wearing them now—in peace, and without a possibility of ever being called upon to take the field. Who, she thought, had ever known the Yeomanry to be called upon to fight, except in the days of his youth of which she had heard her father speak, when the Yeomanry had been pitted agairlst rioters and a few heads and bones had been broken, _ _ . . . .

Lizzie., like 11;t:ft,'Catne of substantial people, and both had profited more or less by the educa- tion they had received in a neighbouring town. In each case the instruction had been belter than that teaching which had come the way of the sons and daughters of the other farmers in the district, and accordingly the two were looked upon with some- thing like jealousy. But they had no actual enemies. Mark was a genial, even, good-hearted farmer, whose soul was mostly wrapped up in Hill Top Farm, and Lizzie was a well-made, healthy young woman with a perfect figure, firm, full sweet lips, steady grey eyes, faultless teeth, and a wealth of brown hair which curled without the aid of tongs or pins. By her own express wish she had been put in the way of earning her living in case of need, and her choice had fallen on nursing. She was home again after a course of work in hospital, and preparing for the marriage which was to make her Mrs. Benn. . .

Mark covered the five miles at a smart trot, and joined his companions at the regiment's head- quarters. During the whole of the ride his thoughts dwelt principally on his father's sneer at the Yeomanry, and his bitterness had returned when he dismounted.

Then he had instant evidence of great commotion. Men were gathered in excited groups, and a comrade ctune and seized Benn enthusiastically by the arm. _ _

• -- "Come on, Mark," he exclaimed. "If you'll go go too," "Go where?" demanded the young man. ▪ "To the war, of course. Where do you think ? " "War?" repeated Benn. "I don't Understand." "Why, chumphcad," said his friend, hilariously, "there's only one war, is there? Or don't they know about it yet at the Green?"

-..-.. - "But—but we're only Yeomanry; we're no use," said Mark, almost unconsciously giving utterance to the faith in which he had been bred. He remembered how in the days of his own youth he had shouted "noodle" after Yeomanry cavalry- men, just as juveniles to-day cried out the insult after him. j _ _

"Ah I" said is friend, but the War Office is In such a funk that it's glad to fall back on even the Yeomanry. It's discovered all at once that we're a very find body of men. You've heard of the way our people in South Africa have been wiped out?" "No," replied Mark, almost shamefacedly. "The fact is, I've been working like a nigger lately. There's been no time to think or read."

"They can't do much of either down your way, judging by what you seem to know of what's going. on," said the friend, genially. "Well, understand this, the country's in a hole, and it's looking to the good old Yeomanry to get it out. The Adjutant's rushing round for volunteers for the front—the .real thing at last, mind you. What I • say is, I'll go if you will. I want a chum. The Captain's going, he's gone out with the Adjutant for a bottle of fizz on the strength of the picnic, as he calls it. What d'ye sty Wiil you go " • The friend gazed eagerly at Mark. "Come, yon won't duff," he added, fearing that the young man might say no.

Mark was thinking of the girl at laskson's p farm, of his mother, of the Hill Top Farm itself, of the wonderful love he had for every field, hedge, tree, building, and every livihg Areature on it; he recalled, as A man might recall a dream that has .passed, the beauty of the scene as he came in from the hayfield, scythe on shoulder. These things flashed through his mind, and he hesitated. But then. he thought of his father's taunt, the taunt which now came back as fresh as ever, and with added bitterness. Before the sting of it all, his love for Liz and his people and the farm collapsed. "Yes, I'll go with you," he said, quietly. "Hurrah I"- shouted his friend. "Look out, there I Make way for two more. Here, sergeant- major, put down the names of Benn and Brigg— two B's that'll have a rousing tale to tell to the other B's in South Africa. Ha, ha!" . _

"That's the ticket, my lads I" said the old regular, looking with an expert's eyb on the new recruits. "You're both as tough as leather. The doctor'll pass yod with • his eyes shut. You're goin' out on a proper bit of sport this time, I cat; tell you. There's never been such a beanfeast in the British Army. Why, you'll be better off than the officers were when I first joined. This is a gentleman's campaign, this is. Certainly. I don't mind if I do—just to bring luck on it." This was in .asponse to the loud request of Brigg that the sergeant-major should there and then proceed to an adjacent inn and celebrate the occasion in whisky. The non-commissioned officer kept one eye on his glass and the other on the headquarters door, so that th e adjutant should not return and steal a march upon him, and held forth on tho glories and privileges of the coming opera- tions, and the singular appropriateness of farmers going out to fight farmers. As a matter of fact, the sermeant-major really believed that the Yeomen were the'. troops for the foe, partly because they could fight them at their own game, and partly because he held that it was somewhat of a degrada- tion for the regular forces of Great Britain— particularly the mounted branches—to be pitted against an enemy who had no orthodox military system, and to whom uniform and discipline were almost unknown. Such, at any rate, was his own private view of the matter.

There was no drill that night. The adjutant took the names of volunteers, with the help of the sergeant-major, and the stars seemed to Benn and Brigg to be twinkling with unnatural violence as they rode together along the high Way. Benn was a rigidly temperate man' and a very little liquor and much excitement had made him reel sus- piciously in his saddle. But the mare knew both him and the road well, and did not allow him to come to grief. "See you to-morrow again," Brigg sang out in the night as he drew up at his father's house. "I'll go straight in and spring it on the governor. You must tell me how yours takes it; I 'xpect mine'll be a bit stunned. He's a chapel chap, you know, and doesn't hold with war." Benn went back to Jackson's 4 a walk. When he reached the house only the old man was up, and he was yawning and very sleepy. He went with Mark to the stable, and having seen the door safely locked and his precious mare secure, he said:

"Good-night, my lad; I'm not goin' to stop up jawin'. I'm fair tired. Liz went to bed an hour since, although she wanted to stop up. She said something about bad news from the war, but I told her that 'ud keep till Sunday. What wi' these new machines for haymakin' an' rogues 'at don't work, it's cappin' what little time there is for me to think about South Africa." Mark's good-night rang on the still, sweet air, and soon his firm step sounded on the gravel in front of Hill Top Farm. The stout old structure was in darkness except for the living-room, through the little panes of which the trooper saw a lamp burning. By the light of it he could see that his father was sitting up waiting for him. This was unusual. As a rule his sister or his mother waited for him when he was out late like this—to their minds ten o'clock was a fit retiring time for the wildest of young men—and he anticipated stormy words. As Mark entered the old man put down his glasses and a book he had been reading., and looked at his son as he secured the door and removed his helmet and accoutrements.

"Your mother's left some supper here," he said. "Get it. There's no need to be up so soon in the morning. Six o'clock will be in plenty of time. I could have spared Prince, after all." "1 borrowed Jackson's mare," answered Mark, stolidly. "She's as hard as Prince." "He's worth two of her," said the farmer, placidly. "However, next time there's a drill, take him."

He spoke without emotion, but this was his way of shewing sorrow for his temper earlier in the evening. "It'll be a long time before I want to borrow Prince again," said Mark, with a curious little laugh.

"What for ? " asked the father, quickly. "I've given up bein' a noodle," replied the young man.

A gleam of pleasure shot into the farmer's eyes. "I'm glad," he said. "It's been a waste o' time. But what have you given it up for ? " "Because," replied Mark, very steadily, "I didn't like being laughed at. I've gone in for the real thing." "'Listed ?" asked the farmer. Ile spoke bravely, but the hand which held the candle he had just lighted trembled. "Volunteered. I'm not sworn in yet," answered the young man. "They're wanting Yeomen for South Africa, and I've said I'll go. "You'll think better of it by mornin'," said the farmer, moving towards the staircase. "There'd be no harm in tellin' the Captain that you'd changed your mind." "I should be laughed at more than I've been laughed at as a noodle," answered Mark; "and that's more than I care to stand." "They wouldn't laugh mg ilk) doin' a thing I didn't want to do," saca. thO farmer, grimly. "But Ido want to do this," said Mark. "After all, there isn't much change in ..a life like mine." "There's been change enough in it for me," replied the old man. "And there's been change enough in it for Benns ever since the house was bniit" "That's likely enough, but then they were an easy lot, and ?:ou didn't catch them going far out of Yorkshire." "They'd too much sense," said the farmer. "They'd all they wanted here. What more do you want?" "I want to see the world a bit," answered Mark. "Besides, it's time this family did something for the country." . .

They Ila been talking like this at the drill hall, and the excitement of it had still a. strong hold upon him. "The Berms paid their way an' kept honest; isn't that enough ?" asked the farmer. "No," replied the son, boldly. "Anyway, that isn't what they think at the barracks." "That's a queer place to go to for opinions. It's been usual to look down on soldiers in this part." "Yes, it's time folk were taught to look up." For some moments the old farmer stood, candle in hand, looking at his son. "And how many fools are goin' ?" lie asked, bitterly. "If you mean how many volunteers, I can't tell you exactly," replied the son, hardening in his resolve to go at all costs now. "An' you're goin' in that rig-out ?" inquired the father. "No, they'll put us into khaki ; it's the best stuff yet discovered for fighting." "Very well, then, if you're really bent on goin', the best thing'll be to get away as soon as you like. I won't stand in your way." "All right,' I'll clear out by the end of the -week." _ . .

The farmer went as far as the staircase. Then he turned back. "Does Lizzie Jackson know of this he asked." . _

"Not yet ; I'll tell her when it's all settled." "Oh, I should have thought that as you're to marry her, you might have asked her opinion." "I've heard you say lots of times that the Benns haven't made a point of consulting women too much, and that it's better to tell 'em of a thing when it's .been done," replied the young man. "That's what I shall do." 'Then I shall tell her first thing in t' mornin'," threatened the farmer. "That won't make any difference," answered the son, quietly. By this time they were on the verge of a dangerous quarrel. The old man saw this, and by an effort kept his passion down. Talk was useless,. and he turned abruptly and went to bed. But ho did not sleep. Hour after hour he lay still, thinking over the events of the night, and con- demning himself as the cause of Mark's determina- tion. He listened, too, for the sound of his son going to bed, but no sound was heard. At day- break the old man, harassed and unrefreshed, got up and went downstairs very quietly. The living.. room looked raw and grey in the growing light. In a chair beside the table was his son, his spurs still on his heels, his tunic open, and his helmet on the table where he had put it the night before.

The lamp was still burning, and the young won't supper—beer and cheese and bread—was untouched. Mark himself was huddled up in the chair, sleeping quietly. The old farmer, motionless, watched the unconscious figure until the sun rose and the early beams stole through the quaint panes. Then he withdrew, without noise, and busied himself in the farmyard. An hour later, while the farmer was standing at the mistal door, Mark, once more in his work- ling clothes, with scythe on shoulder, came past ors his way to the field which was the next to be mown.

The son walked on without looking or speaking; and the old man said no word and made no• friendly sign. •

( To be continued.)

AIWOMAN'S SHADOWED LIFE.

• In a well-kept little house, four miles from Folke- stone, resides Mrs Jane Cole, wife of the respected fore- man of the Folkestone Waterworks. Mrs Cole is thirty-Dine years at age, and for many years has lived In the town aid district of Folkestone. Although novr bright, happy and content, with five sunny children, Mrs Cole still bears sign, of an ordeal she has gene through. Some twelve years ago sbe first felt the un- accountable influence of a great dread, which developed into a fearful nervous feeling. Then came sickness. A dotter was called in, but time went on, and Mrs Cole rapidly grew weaker and more nervous. She was, in fact, seen to be utterly wrecked, so altered that cld friends would hardly recognise her, and in a state of general aollapse. Her ills were intensified by "white lag" (an aggravated form of ar me,ia) : she could not at I this period get her boot on, and walking was entirely out of the question. At this crisis Mrs Cole, who had tried almost every likely remedy, was recommendd by a neighbour to use Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, with the result that she was soon restored to health. At this point we will let Mrs. Cole continue her narra- tive in her own language, as reported in the " Folke- stone Chronicle,' whose specially accredited representa- tive questioned and corroborates her. " I can say," she averred, "that Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people drove away a shadow that threatened my very life, though it was not until I had taken two boxes that I began to feel any lasting bene- fit. By that time my appetite had returned, and I Gould do that which I had not done for many a long and weary day—eat and enjoy agood meal." '• You say your appetite returned. How about the nervousness ? "It was some five or six weeks before that wore off, but now I am a rew creature. The awful fear I was subject to has gone entirely. I told you that in the early days of my illness I could not walk. Nw (the fact speaks for itself significantly enough) I walk to Folkestone and back sometimes twice a week, do my shopping, and often caul: my purchases home with me. I can truly tell you sir, that I bless the day when I first beard of Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people from my neighbour." •

Youri 18 in extraordinary cure undoubtedly, Mrs- Dela • but does your knowledge extend any further as to the benefits derived from this remedy ?" " Oh, yes; a friend of Mille, Mrs Philpott, at Cheriton, saved her baby's life by dissolving the pills in water and administering them in that form to the infant, and I could give you bra of oatnes. To my husband's mother who suffers excruciating new pains,l recommended the pills, and she says that they are the only means by which she obtained relief from pain and which alleviate the nervousness that follows neuralgia. Then, again, you have the case of a lady livirg near the office of the 'Folkestone Chronicle." She has gone through years of suffering, been orerated upon in one of the Metro- politan hospitals and sent home as hopeless. Not long ago I sleet her. She was, to my surprise, her former self again, looking and feeling well and hearty. The first thing she told me was that she owed her recovery entirely to Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people. Mrs. Cole's address is Lower Standen, near Folkes- stone. The oases above related are typical of many which conetautly create local enthusiasm in all parts cf the country. There is scarcely a village, and certainly not a considerable town' which does not contain sound and hearty people who have been saved from a condition of wretched ill-health or permanent invalidism by Dr Williams' pink pills—always the genuine pill,, however, for it is a remarkable fact that though übstitutes are pushed by a large number of tradesmen upon the accept- ance of people whom they think easily deceived, not one cure has ever been reported from the use of a substitute; though, on the other hand, many people have bees permanently injured by them. The best plan when a substitute is offered is to walk out of the shop, disre- garding the false pretences of the shopman who tries to sell substitutes and send direct to Dr tit'Eliams' Medicine company, Holborn-viaduct, London, for the genuine Pills—two *billings and ninepence for one box, thirteen and nine for six. When the wrapper bears the full title. Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people, and then only, the pills are genuine. They have cured impover. imbed blood, amen:lie, rickets, scrofula, " decline," consumption, indigestion, palpitations, rheumatism,. sciatica, St Vitus' dance, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, neuralgia, and all disturbances of the nervous system.,

The annual meeting in connection with this fund was held at the Town Hall, Runcern on Monday after- noon. The number 'lf -persons visited by the nurse during the year was 277. The patients receiving regular visits were 120, v:z :—Medical, 68 ; surgical, SI typhoid, 11. Number of visits, 2,114. Patients receiv- ing ;occasional visits, 157. Number of visits 835. Total number of visits, 2,949. Of the 120, cases visited during the year 61 are convalescent,. 31 have died, 3 have gone to hospitals, 7 to convalescent homes, 6 have been put on the oecasional visits list, and 12 are still on the books. The actual number of vista is not quite so large as last year, but it should be noted that the typhoid cases fell from 41 to 11, and the visits, to typhoid cases from 989 to 259, so that the ordinary visits show a large decrease."

Rhyl v. Newton-le-Willows.

At Rhyl. There was a good deal of interest shown in the game, as it was the first combination match of the season at home. The visitors arrived late. A cross wind was blowing. The visitors started the game. Newton made a good attempt. Immediately after Rhyl scored a grand goal. A foul was given against Rhyl, but proved useless. Robinson cleared well. Nice pass- ing broueht the ball dowa, but Newton cleared. Half- time—Rhyl 6, Newton nil. Final MO 8 Newton 1. First Qualifying Round—English Cup. St. Helens v. Earlestown.

At St. Helens in showery and boisterous weather and before a moderate attendance. The wind blew right across the ground. Earlestown started against the sun, and at first play was unintere3ting, neither aide grt Ang away from midfield. The Town were the first to be prominent and sent past twice. Then the visitors had a look in, but Houghton cleverly bustled Barlow and relieved. The wind veered round in favour of Earles- town, who began to make matters lively for the town. 'Danner came again from the right, and Bartley sent in a capital centre, which was turned ireto the net by the other for w 'Ms. From the kick off the Town got well down, but Walker badly missed a good centre from M'Guffie, while Eccles was pulled up for off,ide. Flay reverted tc midfield, whence the Earlestown quintette suddenly dashed away, Bartley shooting past from short range. Walker was getting well down for St. Eelens when the ball was carried out, and Earlestown came again. Sterrup intervened and served up to M'Guffie, who prettily passed to Roberts. Tom Leigh stopped the run, and Barlow tried a shot from midfield. Hoy negotiated it all right, and at the other end Chew fisted out finely from a scrimmage. Good work by Clibborn helped Earlestown pealwards, but the attack was repelled, and and M'Guffie and Houghton cantered down gaily on the left. The movement ended in Eccles scoring with a grand shot from Houghton's centre. Matters being now level another determined onslaught was made by the Town, but Jack Leigh defended strongly. Limond crashed in a lightning shot which missed by inches. An attack was initiated by CHl:horn and Stirrup was called upon. He transferred to his colleague, and a pretty bout of passing ensued between Houghton, M'Guffie, and Roberts. the latter shooting over. A centre from M'Guffie flashed acres; the goal mouth, and was got away. Both ends were visited in turn, the St Helens men being most dangerous in front of goal. Jack Leigh was offside when in a good posi- tion. Half-time—St Helens Town 1, Earlestown 1. Final S: Helens 2, Earlestown 1.

Death of Local R.S.P,C.A.. Inspectors

The -death occurred at his residenC;iti-irick- street, early on Tuesday morning of ex-Inspector William Brown, who severed his connection with the Warrington Branch of the R.S P.C.A.,some time ago. Decemed had been in ill health for the past twelve months, and had suffered from heart disease and bronchitis. He was in his 46th year, and had been attended by Dr Liston. Inspector Greatrex, Who was formerly stationed at Warrington, died on Tuesday, October Ist, at the Royal Infirmary, Newcastle-on-Tyne. Deceased had been ill for some four or five months, suffering from a cancerous tumour, for which an operation was deemed itecessary. After the operation pneumonia set in. and Mr Greatrex died as stated above. Re was 67 years of age. Mr Greatrex preceded Inspector ;3rown at Warrington.

Strong Complaint from the Vicar of

Burtonwood.

A meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bwsey Chambers on Wednesday aftern,son, Mr Joshua Ball presiding. There were also piesent : Messrs J W Knowles, W Houghton, Mather, Halton. Backhouse, and Dr Murray, with J C Sutton (elerk), S Sutton (assistant clerk), Dr Sephton (mAdical cffher of health), F Kerfoot (inspector), and B S Newcombe (sur- veyor). MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.-DEATH FROM BEER POISONING.

Dr. SErtrrox repornd that during the month there had been 28 births and 10 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 9 9 per thousand against 12 the average of the last three months, and 9 2 the correspending period last year. During the month five cases of scarlet fever had been reported. The DOCTOR said he had trade inquiries respecting a death from peripheral neuritis which occurred in Liver- pool-road. Sankey, and which was reported at the last meeting of the Council. It appeared that death was due to arsenical poisoning brought on by beer drinking. The CLERK said it would be for the doctor in the case to certify the cause tf death and communicate with the registrar. The responsibility of that Council, except for record, was at an end. Tbe report was adopted. _ _ .

-3:111-R-T-ONWOOD 'WATER SUPPLY—A SERIOUS MATTER. The INsPECTOR reported that in accordance with the Council's instructions he had made inquiries respecting the length of the water main required to be laid in the township of Buttonwood from Broad Lane to Causeway Bridge, also the number cf houses on the route. He had ascertained that the number of houses was 19, only 6of which had a supply sufficient for domestic use. The length of main rr-quired to be laid was 1 mile, 7 furlongs, 17 yards. From Chap. Wane to Haslam's farm the length of main required would be 1 mile,7 furlongs, 293 yards. The number of houses on the route was 23,1 13 of which had an insufficient supply. The total length of main required would be 3 miles, 6 furlongs, 310 yards. Mr HOUGHTON said he was afraid that the Parish of Buttonwood would be " playing another tune some day." Mr Forshaw, had, however, said that the parish agreed to the extensions. The CLERK said the responsibility rested upon the I Council. It was only a matter of courtesy that they authorised the extensions. They must not part with the discretion simply because they had the Parish Council's coiniOn. It would be a very serious matter to carry the main such a distance for so small a return. On tbe other hand there was the question as to whether the people had any other means of getting water. It would perhaps be well for the Inspector to see what effect the rain-fall had had. Mr flacrox asked why the landlord—Lord Lilford— did not supply the water. The INSPECTOR We cannot compel him. Mr KNowLas sail that in that case the tenant would have to pay for the water when it was supplied. The Parish Council had sanctioned that the work should be

Mr HALTON did not think the Parish Council bad realized the position. Mr KNOWLE3 sad the Rural Council had agreed at a previous meeting that those who required water should be supplied. He proposed that the extension should be carried out.

The CLERK said that careful deliberation in the matter was very desirable, so that they might see exactly what amount they were going to spend on the matter. Be thought they should have a report on the pounds, shillings, and pence question. (Hear, hear). Mr KNOWLE9 expressed his willingness for the matter to be deferred until such a report was supplied. The matter was accordingly deferred until the next meeting in order that the inspector might make further inquiries and present a report.

- - STRONG COMPLAINT FM THE VICAR OF BURTONWOOD..- " COUNCIL CULPABLY NFGLECTFUL OF ITS DUTY."

The Local Government Board wrote incicsing-COpy of a further letter which had been addressed to them by the 11,m7 A M Mitchell,vicar of Burtonwood and request- ing to be furnished with the observations of the Council on the matter. _ _ _

The letter referred to was addressed to the secretary of the Local Government Board and was on the subject of a nuisance alleged to be caused by a brook in Buttonwood, and which had previously been brought before the notice of the Board by the rev gentleman. It read as follows i— t, Sir, with reference to your letter bearing date 20:h August, I have to report with regret that no abatement of the nuisance referred to in my previous letter has yet been made. The explanation of the Rural District ICouncil is therefore scarcely bona-fide. The brook in question is in a most disgraceful condition and no effort is being made to apply a remedy. The sanitary author- ity is culpably neglectful of its duty. I am yours faithfully. A M MITCHELL." The INSPECTOR reported the results of inspections of the brook which he had made. Oa the 30th ult, thel brook was foul, owing to various causes which he had already reported upon. On the previous day (Tuesday) he was again in Burtonwood. There was a good stream of fresh water passing down the brook which was quite clear. There was no offensive matter and there was no nuisance whatever arising from the brook. Dr SEPRTON Said that at the request of the Coun- cil he met Mr. Forshaw at the brewery last Wednesday and they inspected the brook together. They found it very much polluted, mainly by the sewer- age from the brewery. He (the dcctor) suggested certain alterations in the sewage tank and filter bed at the brewery, which Mr Forshaw readily consented to adopt. Tbe brook required clean- ing out for some distance from the brewery. Dr Sepliton added that Mr Mitchell complained of the brook being polluted below the sewage works. The works were not the cause of the pollution,which proceeded mainly from Mr Forshaw's brewery. If certain alterations were made there, it would improve the brook considerably. The work would in all probability be carried out before the next meeting of the Council. The alterations would prevent any further expense on the township. . .

- - It was decided that the Clerk should s.vrite to the Local Government Baard informing them that the matter was being dealt with, and that a further repast would be presented at the next Council meeting.

P3RT SANITARY AUTHORITY.

Mr BROOKS was appointed to represent the Council as a member of the Joint Cqmmitteo for the Port of Man- cht ster in accordance with the provisions of the Local Government Board Provisional Order Confirmation Act 190 L This concluded the business,

Latchford Imperial Pierrots. On Monday evening the Latchford Imperial Pierrots gave the first two concerts in the Parish Room, Latchford, in aid of the Christ Church En- largement Fund. A capital programme was sub- mitted which opened with the overture "Der Freischutz," by the orchestra. The other items were :—opening chorus, "The so:dier's chorus," the troupe; song, "My daddy's a gentleman," Mr H Houghton; humorous song, "Go and have it out, like I did," Mr F Freeman; song " Out on the Deep," Mr J ; humorous song "One's enough." Mr W Watkins; patriotic song "Volunteer." Mr F Stringer; humorous song," It's not cooked yet," Mr J Corbett; coon song, "Oh Ebenezer," Mr D Knight; humorous song, "I burst out laughing," Mr E Summers; song, "The Kerry dance," Mr R Grounds; song, "Don't you know," Mr .1 Critchley; patriotic Emig, "The flag we love," Mr J Knight ; banjo solo, Mr Kendrick; quartette, the Latchford Quartette Party; grand march, "The Brazilian," the orchestra. The principal item in the second part of the programme was the laughable sketch, entitled My friendly Advisers." HOUSE COMMITTEE.

At a meeting of this committee it was recommended that the tea party proposed to be given by Mies Massy be gratefully accepted. Messrs A Jolley, Pilling,Mather and Robinson be appointed a committee to report upon the laying out of the grounds of the new infirmary. The name of Miss Nancy FAirclough was added to the list of probationary workers.—These minutes were passed. THE APPOINTMENT OF A PUBLIC VACCINATOR. SHOULD HE BE THE RESIDENT MEDICAL OFFICER OF THE WORKHOUSE ?

The Local Govercm'int Board wrote acknowledging the receipt of the Clerk's letter with reference to the proposal of the Guardians of the Warrington Union to combine in one person the appointment of a public vaccinator and resident medical officer of the Work- house. The Local Government Board suggested that the Guardians shou'd consider whether the appointment as public vaccinator of a resident Workhouse medical officer might not be undesirable on the ground that his close a.sociatinn with the Workhouse might deter persons above the rank of paupers from availing them selvcs of his services in the first mentioned capacity. The communication was considered by the House Committee and it Was recommended that in respect of the question asked. the Clerk be directed to reply that the Guardians see no objection to the combination of the offices of public vaccinator and resident medical I officer in one person. I Mr Becomes moved the adoption of the recommenda- tion of the House Committee. Mr MATHER seconded.

Mr PILLING moved an amendment that the minutes of the House Committee be passed with the exception of that relating to the suggested dual appointment of public vaccinator and resident medical officer at the Workhouse. an did so, he said, on the ground that he was of the opinion expressed by the Local Government Board that the two offices would not work well to- gether. Mr J W KNOWLES seconded the amendment. Al the present time, he said, practically the whole of the vaccination of children was done by the pubic vaccina- tor, and the idea of the Local Government Board in fixing the fees on a liberal scale was to retain the services of a leading practitioner in the district as public vaccinator. By combining the office of medical officer at the Workhouse with that of the public vaccinator they would not get a leading practitioner in the district to apply for the position because the salary would not be equal to what they were earning as private practitioners. It would mean that they would get a young man who had had very little practice. He would not care to have a young man experimenting on a child of his. The Bard requiredi 'Elliman of experience at the head of his profession to undertake the important work of vaccination and such a man could be got for the remuneration or fees be would be able to get out of the office. He did not think a workingman, or one in better circumstances would like the stigma of it being said that their children had been Vaccinated by the Workhouse eloctor. A feeling of that kind would be engendered if they amalgamated the two offices as suggested. Mr BILLINGE supported the amendment. The CHAIRMAN said he would like to point out that there appeared to be some misapprehension in the minds of the mover and seconder of the amendment. Mr Pilling said he was of the same opinion as the Local Government Board. As a matter of fact the Local Government Board had not expressed any opinion at I all. They had asked for the opinion of the Guardians. Mr Knowles had said that the position ought to be held by a man of good standing in his profession. On that point the House Committee was entirely at one with him. If they were to have a resident medical officer they wanted to obtain the services of the best man they , could and it was the opinion of the rnajnity of the committee that that encl:mirlit be best affected by combining in one individual tnese two offices. The Guardians were compelled to pay certain' fees out of the ratepayers' money for vaccination, and ' it was thought by amalgamating the two offices they would make the post so valuable that if it were thrown open for competition, they would get an experienced man. As to the talk about experimenting on anyone it was a well known fact that before a medical man was recognised as a public vaccinator;he must be fully quali- fied for his work. The Local Government Board required to be satisfied on that point. Personally he should be sorry if the amendment were carried. Mr KNOWLES: You lose sight of the fact that in these days the duties of public vaccinator and poor law doctor are two different appointments. The CHAIRMAN : All our public vaccinators at present are Union Officers. Mr PILLING : Our last one wasn't. Tee CHAIRMAN Originally he was. Mr KNOWLES : The big fees are given with a view of inducing leading medical men to undertake the duties. Mr CLARKE supported the amendment on the ground that it would be unfortunate to appoint a medical 'officer for the Workhouse and give him vaccination duties outside. reMr BROOKS feaid the Flame Committee wanted a first class man in the Workhouse but at present there was not sufficient work to keep a doctor going. Mr BILLINGE : Who said so? Mr BROOKS : As a member of the House Committee I know that is so. He did not think the Guardians would like to pay the high remuneration necessary to get a good resident medical officer. The schema brought

MR. WINSTON CHURCHILL M.P. AT ST HELENS. Mr Winston Churchill, M.P., on Tuesday night delivered his lecture, " The War as 1 ssw It," at the St Helens Town Hall: The Mayor (Alderman J Forster) presided over a large and influential audience. The lecture was given under the auspices of the Young Men's Christian Assochition, and the committee and officials were invited by the Mayor to meet Mr Churchill in the Mayor's parlour, those present including Sir David ] Gamble, Bart. and Colonel Richard Pilkington, M.P.) with two of his three sons who have served in South Africa. 1 The Mayor briefly introduced the lecturer, and said I that St Beiens was slot behind any other place in the kingdom in its delight to welcome any man who had 1 distinguisted himself so much as Mr Winston Churchill I ] (Applause.) They were always pleased to hear of British pluck, intelligence, and smartness, and they had I I read enough to know that Mr Churchill bad exhibited I all those splendid qualities. Mr Churchill gave a most interesting account of the ] operations in Natal, and concluded by saying that the war was not over yet, and there still rernainedjenough of the heavy task to try their patience, fortitude, and 'tact ; indeed, it was a grievous undertaking, for now that the issue was certain one could not help grudging ' every life sacrificed to futile bitterness, and to those savage passions which war itself aroused. Yet LI would seem that the more thoroughly such quarrels were fought out at the time the better was the understanding between the combatants afterwards, and it was from such conflicts that larger systems and stronger unions rose firmer and more enduring. He cherished the hope that the memories of those battles and ambuscades which now seemed so horrid and forlorn would be among the things which should bind together those two stubborn, war .worn, and apt unworthy races' upon whose friendly co-operation the future of South Africa depended. (Applause). The Mayor moved a vote of gratitude to Mr Churchill 1 who asked the audience to subscribe to the Field Forces Fund. _

Great progress has been made lately with the defence of the Firth of Forth. The fortifications at North and South Queensferry for the defence of the Forth Bridge are well advanced, and are to be equipped with power- ful batteries of heavy guns. Inchkeith has been strengthened, and at Bingham the fortifications and guns have been overhauled, The effect of the arma- ments at Kinghorn, when completed, will be to render the north channel of the Firth (which is the principal channel, and, indeed, the only one open to big vessels) impassable to an enemy. Heavy batteries are to be erected there soon. The Clerk said he had looked up the correspondence they had had with the Parish Council at Norley two years ago. Since then nothing had been done. The first step to be taken shou!d be to approach the landowners—there were two of them—and see what they were prepared to do. It would be an expensive undertaking, but in the event of the landowners doing something handsome they would do their part. He supposed they would have to adopt the Gurney scheme, though there were perhaps other ways which would be cheaper. The matter will again be brought up for further nor- sideration.

The Study of a Mad World. h.

Sir,—Where ignorance is bliss 'tis folly to repudiate Mr Chamberlain. Let us, then, by all means, support him. Let us, then, by strenuous effort, continue, like Aaron, to hold up the hands of this up-to-date Moses, that his people may still be ignorant—and happy! Amongst all the fhwers that blossom in that Cimmerian hot-h,ouse at Wesminster, truly Moses is the rarest orchid! His price as an orchid is inestimable because his form is extraordinary and novel. Sever before aid orchid assume such a shape. He is like the monstrous vision of some distempered Ez ,kiel—a figure with the face of a man—a nose like a self-driving screw—a stony eye like a sphynx propounding riddles—a Brummagem jaw—a cheek like unto the cheek of that most pathetic figure which may any day be discerned on the tanks of the Bridgewater Canal, to wit—a boat-horse' That syhynx was once a Radical ! That sphynx once pro- mised three acres and a cow! That sphynx hinted at old-age pensions. But that sphynx ever had one flash- ing binocular for the people and one undimmed eye for the other side! When the obese Homeric autocrat dies —when the sophist nephew of Homer has resigned for a little period—that sphynx shall become Prime Minister! Nevertheless, by ignorance, he arose, and by blissful ignorance, he continueth. For, as the Lord maketh the people he paireth them with rulers. So Be bestowed Mr Joseph Chamberlain—the one and only Unitarian— the mighty contractor—the friend of Rhodes and of jazneson—upon the worthy Biitish nation! The British nation, we repeat, is ignorant, and where ignorance is bliss tis folly to be otherwise ! England is happy in the following imaginations. She believes that the war of an elephant upon a nest of field-mice, as Mark Twain says, is glorious. She believes that the march of concentrated and iron battalions, through guerilla fighters to a guerilla capital is one of the most briniest achievements on record. She believes that the

A LANCASHIRE CASE FOR INSTANCE.

A Newspaper Investigation of a Woman's Remarkable Cure.

An affair ha 5 come to light at Hulme, which is regarded in the neighbourhood with quite extraordinary interest. A Manchester reporter ran across the incident in question, and obtained an exclusive story._ _ _

Mrs. Elizabeth Harvey, forty-one years of age, resides at No, 45 Owen Street, Hulme, Man— chester, and the reporter interviewed her a few days ago at her residence. Mrs. Harvey's exact words are given as taken down by the reporter. "I have turned forty-one years of age," she said, "and I have been troubled with a cough and chronic pain in the chest ever since I can remember. The doctors said it was bronchitis. I got so bad that three years ago I was induced to attend: the Hospital for Consumption and Diseases of the Throat and Chest, in Hardman•street, Deansgate, as an out patient. The least exertion made me very short of breath, and I was many times unable to attend to my household duties. I became very weak, as I had no desire to eat and did not enjoy my food. The racking cough was very distressing, and the pain in my chest was often almost unbearable. In fact, I was altogether in a very miserable condition. The doctors examined me, and prescribed for my complaint. They gave me medicine and supplied me with liniment to rub on my chest." "I took a lot of medicine, but it did me no good. I got no better, and I had the same pains in my chest. I got tired of going to the hospital, for the treatment did not benefit me at all ; at any rate I got very little relief. At times, I was so short of breath that my family became alarmed for fear I should die of suffocation."

"And then what did you do, for you seem alright now ? "

" It is now over two years ago that a Mr. Grierson, whom I knew, and who had himself receive great good from taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, prevailed upon me to try a box. I had not much faith in them at first, but I thought I would give .rs. HARVEY (from a photo.) them a fair trial. I went on taking them regularly. The first and second boxes did not produce any very tangible results any more than the hospital medicine, but after the third box I experienced a decided change for the better." " What was the nature of the change they effected ? "

"My cough became easier, and the pains in my chest gradually went away. I also got stronger every day, became more cheerful, and could go about my household duties with more pleasure. Work then became no trouble to me. I have taken them at intervals ever since. lam taking them now. Whenever I feel that I require them I take one or two and I am better in health at the present time than I have been for years."

All these things England believes ana is happy, To dissipate her ignorance would be to dis- turb her besotted bliss—to repudiate Mr Coamberlain ! Before the war she was in no danger as a power, even it South Africa had ceased to be her own, which, with ' diplomacy it never would have been. She was at the very height of her glory with no weak place in her armour except lreland—respected and honoured of nations—and worthy of every lofty title which our mighty fathers achieved for us ! Now,through , her folly she stands upon the brink of a precipice ! Killed by anxiety, she has lost her Qaeen—that great personality, who, above all Woes, guaranteed the good- will of the monarchies of Europe. She has wholly diverted her attention from domestic reform at a time when djmestic reform is peculiarly needed. She has given the last stab to Liberalism. She has made straight the way for Oligarchy. She has undermined , her constitution. She his shackled her hands with a task which she can never accomplish. She is daily growing weaker in prestige, power and commerce whilst her rivals over reach her. She has lost in China what the future will decnon.strate to be of infinitely more value than the two Beer republics. She is still inebriated and • nothing but calamity will move her. Calamity must surely come. Seeing this, as a little Englander and true lover of my country I almost man- age to get opinions ! Expression of them,however fails me, and I am compelled to sign myself as before, Yours sincerely,

Organization of Cheshire Farmers. In the course ot a brief address on Monday night at Hyde. to the members of the Hy.le and District Farmers' and Milk Sellers' Association, Mr Edward Chapman, M.P„ emphasized the importance of organization and unity. As dairy farmers they do well to foster an association such as the one under whose auspices they were met ; there was nothing so excellent and necessary as pure milk, and an association like theirs could give a guarantee that the supply was all. that coull be desired.—Mr Sadler, of Nantwich, gave an interesting account of the organization and objects of the Cheshire Milk Producers' Association, of which he is secre- tary Ihis association now numbered, he said, between 900 and 1,000 members, but there were still many remaining outside the pale of the association. Farmers had been very slow to recog- nize the advantage of organizing for the protection of their own interest,but at last they were awaken- ing to the desirability of such a step. He was satisfied that if they did not organize to defend their proper interests a large number of them ' would have to go out of the business of da;rying. They as farmers wanted to sell the public pure milk, an article entirely free from disease germs. but they did not want to be harassed in their busi— ness in an improper manner when they were trying to do their best ; and the only way they could get protection was by forming themselves into a strong body. lithe tenant and dairy farmers of England could only realize their strength they need have no fear of anybody taking undue advantage of them. (Applause.)

The more that the Die Ameer's Death, career of the A:smo

• ABDUR RAHMAN is con- sidered, the more will anxiety be felt as to the events of the next few years in Afghanistan. Tbe late AMEER has been an active figure in the history of his country for twenty years. His natural shrewdness and strength of character, together with the troublous experiences of his early youth/ combined to form the kind of ruler most fitted for the pemliar circumstances of Afghanistan, which found in him one of its greatest Ameers. It sysn well for Great Britain and for India that our Government was able to make such a fortunate j selection of a successor to SHERB Aar. The position of Afghanistan as a buffer State between India and the Russian territory in Central Asia necessitate a strong ruler who can keep a firm hold over the numerous tribes of which its population ie constituted,and at the same time has the wirdoe to maintain an ,attitude of benevolent neutrala towards the two great States which lie on either side. It has at all times been a trying ordeal for the Ameers, and ABDUR RAHMAN was not alwaYs above suspicion. As the pensioner of Russia for many years before he came into his kingdom, was feared that his leanings might be in the dire' tion of favouring the Muscovite Power; but it is probable that during his sojourn in Russiso Turkestan ha saw enough of his hosts to form n° very high opinion of them. At all events, after he became the British nominee for the throne of his/native country, and was duly subsidised, be remained as loyal to the British Government as is possible for an Oriental potentate to become. Although trouble arose over the fighting with the hill tribes of Northern Indi: to whom the AMBER was suspected o' rendering secret sssistance, there it nothing to show bad faith on the part of ABDUL. fact seems to be, as he earnestly explained, tbo the inhabitants of these highland regions never have been subdued, and never will under the Pre- sent order of things. It is indeed the prevalence of this ungovernable disposition on the part of the numerous tribes of the buffer State that makes the transition of power from one ruler to another a matter of so much risk. The intractable dispel' tion of the tribesman is ever ready to shew it in fresh outbreaks when an opportunity ocean!' When the situation is further complicated by the existence of five different claimants to the throne out of the sixteen sons of the late AmEna, tbe prospect is full of the promise of trouble. A. SPECIAL interest Mr Herbert Gladstone attaches to anything and Liberal Prospects. MR HERBERT GLAD. STONE may say on the question of the political situation, because, as the Whip of the Liberal party, he may be held to possess the confidence of the party with which he is so closely associated. He presents a new claim, indeed, to the earnest consideration by Liberals of his utterances on questions of party prospects and policy when he tells us, as he did at Leeds, that he does not in- tend to make his approaching marriage an excuse for throwing up his arduous, and often thankless office of party whip. There is evidence of hope for the future of the party in this determination to stick to it in its darkest hour of adversity. There is more. Mn GLADSTONE shows that he is in love with his work, and has enough of his father's spirit and determination to be anxious to see the party through its difficulties. With the eye that has a long vision, and a broad view, he sees that periods of war are essentially inimical to Liberal unity and effectiveness in politics. That is so in the very nature of things, and he sees no reason to ridicule his party, or any section of it, because it cannot achieve the impossible. War has in it that which absorbs the attention and in- terest of the people to the exclusion of matters affecting them more nearly, and while it lasts domestic concerns are allowed to stand aside. In such a state of events Toryism naturally flourishes and Liberalism becomes the drag on the coach. But while as a party Liberals can do little or noth- ing. they can at least . wait—and wait in the patient hope that their time will come. In this attitude Mr. GLADSTONE finds ample ground for hope that in the not distant future Liberalism will again come to the front as the popular influence in the country. Already, he says, the country wants an alternative Government; but the war must be over first. This throws light on his former statement, which was much criticised at the time, that a Liberal Government at present is impossible. The present Government must end tho muddle they have begun. Mr GLADSTONE is shrewd enough to see, what Unionists are now beginning to fear, that however the war is ended there will not be much credit accruing to the Government in the transaction. By the time it is really over the country will have quite made up its mind for a change of Government. Mr qLADSTONE'S pro posal is to let it do so. He points out that his position is sound from the facts which he formerly stated, that the present House of Commons has a majority of 233 Unionists and Irish over Liberals, and a majority of 120 Unionists over Liberals and Irisn. Such figures are a sufficient proof that a Liberal Government is not within the range of possibility at present. Not that the party is deficient in talented and capable men, but that the latest expression of opinion given by the country, so lately as a year ago, was decidedly hostile to a Liberal regime. A. Liberal Government could not exist a fortnight Some day, perhaps, when there are without a strong expression of favour- lawyers in the House of Commons, some s,tritbe able opinion in the constituencies; but to appeal movement will be made for a codification 000 to the country is still premature. further defined by the Appeal Courts. It would need to be done only once, until a fresh growth of legislation had obscured the points ; but it would be productive and profitable. Such contention, however, would be in everybody's interest, except that of the new generation of lawyers; and to meet their grievance it is only necessary to warn superfluous young men with legal minds to emi- grate to South Africa.
