Last Friday's yacht race decided the whereabouts cf the America Cup for another twelve months. It will remain where it has been for the last half century—in charge of Uncle Jonathan. We have been fairly beaten, and must candidly admit that after winning three races in weather of varied Character the Columbia is the better boat. It seems probable that the construction of racing Yachts has reached the very highest point of perfection for Mr. Herreshoff was unable to improve on Columbia and Mr. Watson, the clever designer of Shamrock 11, proved unequal to the task of building a boat as good. Britishers everywhere will sympathise with Sir Thomas Lipton, whose plucky sportmanshih deserved a better reward. If he challenges again, and we hope he will try a third time Sir Thomas might suggest that the race be sailed in English waters. The home and away system of engagements works very well in football and why should it not in yacht racing ?

A handsome and fitting recognition of the generous treatment meted out to them at home has been made by the Warrington members of the Volunteer Service Company South Lancashire Regiment. It consists of a loving-cup made upon the base of a 1:21b naval shell appropriately inscribed and designed in silver, which was presented to the Mayor and Corporation last week. Such a testimony as this to the good feeling between the civil and military authorities is very gratifying, and redounds greatly to the credit of the Service Volunteers, who in ad- dition to being gallant soldiers are evidently men capable not only oi appreciating kindness but reciprocating in a generous manner.

A Provincial Letter Irrespective of the Board of Trade returns,which have appeared this week, there are many indica- tions if one cared to collate them that trade is de- clining, although one ventures to think that the Pessimist people who are so anxious to forewarn us are taking an unnecessarily exaggerated view of certain facts, and are neglecting to loak at trade all round. Of course, these experts may be evincing en intelligent anticipation of events. Trade is fickle and its tendencies are not always detected at the beginning. Looking at the Board of Trade re- turns, not only for the last quarter but for a much longer period, evidences will undoubtedly ba found of an approaching decline, and it may be as well to begin preparing for it. the receipts of the railway companies for goods traffic have to be looked at as another sure indication ; and, if any further evi- dence be required. the readeris referred to statistics relating to the coal trade. But what the whole country now wants, and wants badly, is to get the war over and its object completed. Meanwhile, there are not lackiogl certain reasons for believ- ing that the check upon trade shown in the Board of frade report is but temporary. They are slender, Perhaps, but need not be forgotten or overlooked. --- A decided fillip has been given to the discussion on the subject of Shop Hours by the publication this week of the full text of 'the report of the Lords' Committee on the Early Closing of shops. The matter has been placed upon a somewhat higher plane by this Committee than that which it formerly occupied, so that the curtailment of hours is now advocated as much in the interest of trades- men as chat of their assistants. The practical recommendation is that Town Councils and Urban Authorities be authorised to pass provisional orders, making such regulations in regard to the Closing of shops as may seem to them to be neces- sary for the areas under their jurisdiction," and :AFen these provisional orders be submitted to Par- liament in the usual manner before acquiring the icrce oi law. The advantage here would be that each community will have the matter in its own hands, to the extent of being in the position at election times of determining what is good for it or otherwise in this respect, so long as Parliament Showed no disposition to interfere with their sug- Rested method of " home rule,' or rather shop rule. For it is very well known that the needs of certain town Tin the provinces, as regards shop hours, are Materially different from those of other towns. 80me have already made great advances in this direction, while others have not advanced a step.

Figures published on the eve of our entering upon the Ttard Year of the South African war are sad reading for the beads of families scattered over the country, while in one respect there is just a grain of consolation. For it is wonderful to notice. in glancing over the list, the large proportion of the men who were wounded and have since been enabled to rejoin their regiments.

As with motors and motorists, so with cycles and cyclists, among those who were not "to the manner born are found many sturdy, and perhaps a few bigoted, opponents. It is in the highest degree improbable that the horse will ever be superseded by any of what enemies may choose to describe as " these new fangled " methods of locomotion. ' Looked upon from a military standpoint, the cycle appears co have both friends and foes in those to whom the people look up as experts. While on the one hand General Douglas,who made the cyclist corps at Aldershot his particular study,thinks that ' with training it is possible to employ, with advan- tage cyclist troops in large bodies in this country, on the other hand General Buller said in his recent report, that ," be looked upon the cycle as the worst and most cumbrous means of transport for soldiers that he had ever seen." The extent, too, of the interpretation to be placed upon General Douglas's report, now icsued, Probably iS that the cyclist can only justify his existence as a military unit in a country wehre the roads are good. Indeed, General Douglas seems almost to go out of his way occasionally to confirm the disquieting impression left on the mind by General Boiler's previous strictures ; and the affect Of these criticisms will probably be to put the Machine thoroughly on its trial, in order to ascer- tain the full extent of its capabilities after the Members of a particular corps have been subjected to a drastically revised system of training. Any- bc'w. the public will scarcely feel satisfied now Until something of the kind has been done, and Meanwhile those companies of volunteers throutzh- cut the provinces with cycle corps attached thereto, Will be anxious to know what the result of it all is tube

----- Mention of motors and cycles tempts one a little oUt of the way to notice Mr Gibson Bowles's decid- edly funny attack upon the Government, which he 'Was returned to support, in the " Times" of Monday last, anent what another Conservative ember has described as the" drifting" of the war In South Africa. His serious paint is that Lord liitchener could have made peace last March, but that Mr Chamberlain ordered changes whiph Lord Milner regretted, and to which Lord Eitchener was even more strongly opposed; and so, he -says, the , 'War goes on, while as for the Cabinet, "its members have gone calmly away to Beaulieu, to Scotland, to Newmarket, taking their leisure, their golf, their motor-cars, and their horse racing • tia though wholly indifferent to a perilous national emergency." Probably at no period of the war has there been so much conflict of opinion on the posi- lott of affairs as there is now, but if it can be regarded as having a serio-comic side "Tommy Coe I, , wies, as he is familiarly designated in the "QOllSe, is the one to afford it.

Advocates of registration reform have long Pointed the finger of scorn at our Revision Courts, aad described them as the happy hunting ground Of the paid agentiand the arena in which to indulge In Petty quibbles and wrangle over all kinds of technicalities. Ev9ry year, when the time forholding the Courts comes round, cases arise Which give force and point to the paradox that the :resent system has the effect of deciding not so '4llCh how the voter's name may be placed on the

register as how it may, "by hook or by crook," be kept off. When dissatisfied critics are backed by I revising barristers themselves the hope is, of course, strengthened that soon the question will be asked in: all seriousness if the courts should ' not be swept completely away, , and something more rational be substituted for them. At one of the courts the other day the revising barrister I spoke of certain claims to be placed on I the register as " preposterous," and yet admitted that by the law he was obliged to allow them. Commenting on another point, the strictly legal 1 way of presenting objectiors, he cited one or two illustrations of the working of the statute, which he said he thought would justify the use of the I same word.

Lodgers for some years occupied most of the time of the revising barrister in some of our Re- vision Courts, but Mr Garland's raid upon them in the Torquay Court.and he is the learned gentleman already Ereferred to —is perhaps the simplest and most effective in its way, whatever its effect in other respects equally important may be. For he laid it down at the outset that one room in a £lO house cannot be worth more than all the rest of the building, and consequently, unless there were special circumstanCes,he would not allow the claim in respect of houses of less than £l6 ratable value. A surprising large number of applications were made in which husbands claimed to be the tenants of their wives, and some even produced rentbooks showing that they had paid their wives as such. Those who did so had their claims allowed, but several other cases are expected to be further argued in the Court of King's Bench !

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A COBRESPONDENT.I On Tuesday the Rural Council had a very ob- noxious pill to swallow in the shape of a sharp, curt note from Dr. Penny stating that he had nothing further to add to his report and that prompt payment would oblige. The note and account of sixty-five guineas produced some small amount of merriment among the worthy Councillors when read by the clerk, but it is a significant fact that no one remarked at Dr Penny's refusal to make a report on Dr Adams's criticism. The Council have made a bad bargain, which has cost them dear, and it is a case of "least said soonest mended."

Mr Naylor is determined to give no rest to the , Council until they do " something " respecting the Thelwall nuisance. Reports will not mollify him and like the woman and the unjust judge he is evi- dently hoping that by his continual writing be will weary them. For the space of a month he has thoughtfully issued to Mr Ashton, the Clerk, daily bulletins respecting the state of the nuisance. Under this paper pressure two or three of the mem- bers have been goaded into a feeling of mind and body perilously akin to the man who felt to re- lieve himself, that he " bad to do something; it I didn't matter what, so long as it was something." Mr Naylor will probably find out before long that the matter is now engaging the attention of the Local Government Board and it will be for them to inquire into the origin of the nuisance. Their repu- tation for promptness and despatch is so well known that he may rest assured the matter is in safe bands and will receive attention probably within the next decade:

One of the members laid down a principle re nuisances which would,if carried out,be vicious in practice as well as being illogical, and opposed to all the canons of argument. Recurring nuisances, he said, " could not have the constant attention of the Council." If a recurring nuisance is the very reason why it should not have constant attention, then, by a similar piece of reasoning, a nuisance which occurs but once should have the Council's constant attention. The sentiment is not only absurd but unworthy of any public man. The more frequently the necessity arises for the Coun- cil to act, the oftener it ought to act, and not by a specious fallacy convince itself. that as a difficulty has been confronted once,and its negotiation failed, it should never try again. The tenet is obviously wrong in theory, and is radically wrong in practice.

It is now almost certain that there will be a School Buard election. The supporters of the Board schools cannot see for one moment why man- agers of voluntary sehools should have the con- trolling voice in the administration of its most powerful rival—the Board school. At present, practically, both classes of schools—rural estab- lishments though they be—are run by the same coterie, which many contend is not only unbusi- ness-like but absolutely unfair. This is one of the points at issue and it is C point that deserves the careful consideration of the electorate. The vol. untarists, on the other hand contend that as they pay rates they have a right to representation. Thus the question lies and the probability is, that the ratepayers will have to answer it at the polling booth. Both parties appear to be quietly muster- ing their forces for the coming struggle, and before bleak December arrives Runcorn should have passed through the throes of an interesting and exciting struggle.

The keynote of what should prove to be a most interesting and instructive discussion was struck at the last meeting ef the School Board on Thurs- day evening. It will be remembered that some time ago Mr Imison asserted that as the precept on the overseers was less than the corresponding amount last year there was ground for congratula- tion. This statement Mr Norman challenges and asks if £1,709, the precept for one year, produces a rate of 4i in the how can .£2,100, the precept for a following year, still produce a4O rate. This , is the main question at issue but there is every reason to believe that the whole ground of the Board's work will be traversed in the coming discussion. Thus there will be much food for reflection when the Board dissolves. Messrs Norman and Imison are such Rupe.rts of debate, and both gentlemen being anxious to arrive at a common understanding, the ratepayers are sure to have their darkness lighten- ed The die•mssion will take place in the first week of November, and ought to be eagerly followed by educationalists.

Runcorn covered themselves with glory on Satur- day in their initial engagement with Hunslet. To a certain extent the visitors were considerably handicapped owing to the loss of their baggage which went astray on the journey to this benighted locality. Still the excuse of" new boots, jerseys, and pants "—which had to be obtained locally— can hardly explain away the severe castigation which the" Myrtles" inflicted on their opponents. The result was an eye-opener for the Yorkshire contingent and they will not forget in a hurry their visit to Cheshire. The game, as a whole, was a very fine one and the early stages seemed to in- dicate that whoever won would do so by a small margin only. The visitors were lucky in winning the toss but even with this advantage they failed to make any impression in the first half. At the change of ends there was only one club in it and it was not liunslet.

The only wonder is that the defeat was not more Crushing. Had Runcorn made the most of their opportunities the score would have been a record for it was patent to everyone that in the last half hour of tbe game the visitors were completely de- moralized. Their forward play was not equal to the Cheshire Champions and in the other depart- ments they were not at all well balanced. Their combination was egregiously at fault and but for Goldthorpe,who played a dashing uphill game,their humiliation would have been complete. Houghton for the winning side, came out strong and put in one or two useful runs one of which was instrumental in effecting a • score. The • übiquitous Jolley was also well to the front and did not spoil his play with overdoses of kicking and marking. With Butterworth,however rest the honours of the day. Not only waft his play neat but it was also effective. His running to earth of the visiting beck which relulted in a score was a fine piece of play as the spectators were not slow to acknowledge. Wilkinson too played a good game his defensive powers showing up very well. The forwards played a splendid game and there was a capital understanding between the whole of the team.

To-day (Saturday) Runcorn play Oldham away. If the day is fine and the form of both clubs good, it should be a great game. Runcorn are sure to make a bold bid for the lead and ought to manage It. There is not much to choose between the teams and, given a small bit of luck, Runcorn will win though on an opponent's ground.

The following team will represent Runcorn on Saturday against Oldham :—S Houghton, back; S Wilkinson, T Warder, J Butterworth and J noran ,

BODY FOUND IN THE RIVER AT WIDNES.

The body of a man was found in the river on Tuesday, on the Hale shore, embedded in sand, and was brought out with the greatest difficulty by the Ditton and Halebank police. It appears to be that of a man of about fifty years of age, no moustache or beard, apparently brown hair, about sft Gin in height, brown worsted jacket and vest, corduroy breeches, tied at the knee in the navvy fashion. ALABINI OF FIRE AT RUNCORN. On Wednesday morning the inhabitants of Run- corn were alarmed by what appeared to be the loud blowing of the fire buzzer. It was rumoured that there was a large fire at Weston Salt Works but in- quiries elicited the fact that the peculiar blowing was caused by a vessel passing up the Ship Canal. Several firemen were deceived by the noise and turned out only to find there was " nothing in the wind."

ACCIDENT TO A CHILD.

At 5.30 on fi;d-n-e;1;37-a-fte-rn-o-O-n—a—little boy and girl were observed by P C. Edwards clinging to the rear of a lurry belonging to William Baker, "Grapes Inn," Halton-road. By some means or other the boy became entangled in the wheel. The constable at once went to his assistance, and together with Dr Robinson, who happened to be passing, removed the boy to his home, where it was found that no bones had been broken, but his ankle had been badly bruised. His name was Arthur Wilding, son of Joseph Wilding, 87, Halton- road.

At the Marsh Farm Halton on Tuesday afternoon IMr R Dobson, coroner, held an inquest touching 1 the death of a farm labourer named John Finch, who died suddenly on Monday at noon. The evi- dence showed that the man was taken ill on Sun- ay when he went to the stables of a local butcher, who finding him ill gave him some stimulants. He stayed at the stables until Monday morning but being no better he was re- moved to his brother's house, Mersey Farm, where he died. Dr Carruthers stated that he hart made a post-mortem examination and death was due to fatty I degeneration of the heart. A verdict in accordance with the medical evidence was returned:

A COSTLY THEFT.

On Friday at the Runcorn Police Court before Messrs E Inaison and R H Posnett, Patrick Whelan was charged with stealing sixpence belonging to Henry Robinson Hall, Prospect Inn, Weston. Annie Harrison, barmaid, said that on Thursday morning at about 7 o'clock prisoner clime and asked for a • drink. As he said he had no Money,witness refused to serve him. A few minutes afterwards when she was upstairs witness heard the money in the drawer being jingled, On entering the bar she saw prisoner leaning over the counter with his baud in the till. ' When questioned by witness as to what he was doing he told her to" mind what she was saying." Witness acquainted her empl.,yer, who was asleep at the time,—Sergeant Gregory proved appre- hending the prisoner who admitted the theft after much equivocation,—Prisoner was sent to gaol for six weeks with hard labour. The Madrigal Tonic Solfa choir, Musical Society and Philhar- monic have each come into existence within the last quarter of a century, had their successes and their failures and died of an inanition one after the other. st is theretore, cause for satisfaction that the town has one musical organisation left full of 1 vigorous and healthy life. This is the Orchestral Society, which by its performances time and again has given delight to thousands of people. The mem- bers opened their winter campaign on Wednesday evening by a concert in Bethesda schoolroom and were encouraged by an audience whi&o filled every available corner. The programme wass composed of items of the" lighter vein," and was enjoyed to the utmost if outward demonstration of the audit- ors may be accepted. The hand opened with Rossini's " fancredi," and also executed waltz, " Estudiantina," Waldtenfel ; American sketch. " Down South," Kappey; selection from "Patience," Sullivan ; polka, " The Clowns," Allier ; and the march, " Coon Drum DI ajor," Stuart. Guided by the baton of Mr Horace W Handley, conductor, to the Society, the instrumentation was of the most finished character, and deserving of the unstinted praise bestowed. Mr George Collins was encored for the clarionet solo " Consin' thro' the rye," rendered in clever style. Vocal harmony was contributed by Mr W Hopwood, whose cultured voice was heard to great advantage in the songs "Oh, oh, hear the wild winds blow," and" Shade of the palm," a recall rewarding each effort. Mr J S Pierce well sustained his reputation as a humorist, and each of his items was encored. ' Altogether the concert was most successful.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held on Thursday last week. Present. Messrs E Imison J.P.C.C. (in the chair), F J Norman S Taylor, W Handley, J Littler, J W Cunningham, Rev J Chambers, J W Light- burn (clerk), and A Ligotburn (deputy clerk).

„ THE SCHOOL MANAGEMENT COMMITTEE. The above Committee recommended that accounts amounting to £263 14. s 61 be passed for payment ; that the payments to Mr J F Moulton and Miss A MlKilourn, in lieu of the percentage of Government grant for specific subjects, be deferred for consideration at a meeting of a commitee to be convened by the Clerk; that Freda Branch be admitted a probationer at the Granville-street school, and Ethel Collinge at the Mill Brow school, both without salary until they become pupil teachers. Passed on the proposition of the Chairman and Mr Taylor. THE RATING QUESTION. After a little consultation it was decided that the matter should be considered at the next meeting of the Board November 7th. _

Mr Norman then.enunciated the points at issue. He said that at the meeting in question Mr Liaison was re— ported to have stated that as the call on the overseers was £5O less than last year there was ground for con- gratulation that the expenditure had decreased. His (the speaker's) contention was that there was no ground at all for that and in the irregular discussion which followed Mr Imison pointed out that what he either said or intended to say was that the precept was less than the last precept only for the previous six months. The other point was this. He (Mr Norman) produced at that meeting an official balance sheer of the Board for the year 1897, the laat year of Mr Pritchard's chairmanship. From that report it appeared that £1,700 was drawn from the rates which it was stated at the ensuing election represented qi on the poor rate. For the current year the precepr exceeded £2,000 w hich Mr Imison then stated meant 41 in the £. Be (the speaker) asked Messrs Taylor and Ellison how that could be se3ing that the rateable value of the town, remained practically tbe same. The chairman asked that each member be furnished with a copy of the enumerated points. The Clerk asked the chairman and Mr Norman what facts they would like him to prepare for them. Mr Norman said he did not require any. When he spoke at that meeting he quoted from the official balance sheet which he would again bring forward. The Chairman said he wished it to be understood that the discussion must nor be confined to one year. The three years must come under review. He asked Mr Lightburn to submit to the Board the three balance sheets prior to that and the three balance sheets subsequent. It should not be a limited inquiry and they should go in the matter thoroughly to avoid confusion, and arrive at an understanding agreeable to everybody. Conflicting statements of facts did not add to their dignity. Mr Norman said he was not satisfied. It was wander- ing over a number of years. He (Mr Norman) wished to deal with specific statements made by the Chairman. The Rev J Chambers said it appeared as though both were willing to thrash out the points in question' and to go farther as the necessity of tne case demanded. The meeting then concluded.

RETURN OF A RUNCORN RESERV!ST

We are getting accustomed now with the familiarity which breeds contempt to the return of the brave soldier lads who have done so much for the last two years in upholding the ancient prowess of Great Britain. When a local lad returns from the South African veldt we do not honour and fete him as much as we might do but we nevertheless feel proud that Runcorn has had the distinguished privilege of rearing him even though we do not go to the trouble of telling him so. It is not generally known that Private Antrobus belonging to the 5,b South Lancashire Mounted Infantry arrived in Run- corn after eighteen months hard fighting in South Africa on Sunday last. His neighbours, however, learned of his home-coming and gave him a right royal welcome in their own humble way. It is interesting to know I that Private Antrobus joined his Company—a Mounted Infantry one—in December 1899 when the reservists were called up. His company on arriving in South Africa were attached to a force under General Wynn which took an active part in the memorable struggle at SpionlKop. Shortly after journeying to Vaal Kranty they engaged the enemy for three days. Three weeks, rest at Chievely found the Company fit for the never to be forgotten struggle at Colenzo where the Company distinguished themselves by taking a foremost Part in the capture of Hussar Hill. The Tugela being , then negotiated they journeyed as far as Groblers Kloof but, as every one knows, the Tugela had, shortly after to be recrossed. Private Antrobus also took an active part in the conflicts round Pieters Hill and Double Warner. He had also the distinguished honour of participating in the glorious march to Ladysmith and ,has a vivid recollection of the saddening yet stirring sights;enacted subsequent to the relief of the beleaguered town. Since that historical event the company has been engaged in Dakota Land and checking the inroads of the Boers into Zululand. Private Antrobus looks well and hearty and seems none the worse for his stirring adventures. He has had many thrilling escapes but is I naturally reluctant in speaking about them.

===== 0002601_19011011_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD ELECTION PROSPECTS.

It is now almost certain that there will be a School Board Election in November. Rumour has it that both sides are working quietly but strenuously. The Voluntary party have been silently organising for some 4 time and the ' Progressives have already commenced to work in earnest. The contest has every appearance of being an exceptionally stiff one and will doubtless tend Ito the educational betterment of the electorate. The supporters of the Board Schools intend, it is believed, to run five candidates,and it is also stated on good authority that the Voluntarists also intend to run five gentlemen irrespective of the Catholic representative. This, if correct, will give an addi- tional interest to the struggle. The, names of several prominent local gentlemen are mentioned as likely candidates as wall as the present members of the Board. 8o far, however, nothing can be stated with certainty as to who is likely to step forward for local honours on either side. Doubtless before a week has elapsed a definite pronouncement will be made by both sides as to who the candidates are and for whom the taxpayer must record his vote. The education question is the greatest problem which the British nation has to face and it there-

fore deserves the earnest consideration of that class who can be truly said to guide the trend of public opinion and who thus help to mould the destiny of the Empire. It is something more than a mere local question for the subject assumes national im- portance and the electorate will do well to ponder , the question in all its bearings before committing itself to any decided course of action.

Arbitration Proceedings.

The Increasing Value of Central

Property.

At the Town Hall, Wharington on Mondaymbitration proceedings were commenced with reference to a claim for £19,000 by Mr. Thomas Hewitt, of Southport against the Corporation for premises in Bridge-street which are required to continue the widening of Bridge— street. The premises in question are new occupied by Messrs Hodgkinson, Limited, and Messrs Johnson Bros:, dyers in Bridge-street and there are also buildings at the back including the Eagle and Child Hotel and the stables &c. adjoining. Mr John White was the arbitrator for the claimant and Mr William Wilson for the Cor- poration. Colonel: Bridgford, of Manchester sit as umpire. Mr EEI Lloyd, of Chester (instructed by MEssrs Robert Davies and Company) stated the case for the claimant and Mr E &Mon and Mt T F Byrne (instructed by the Town Clerk) were the counsel for the Corporation. Among others present were Alderman Fairclough, Alderman Sutton, Councillors Bennett and Smethurst, Mr S Wilson (deputy town clerk), Mr J Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr W H Woodcock (assistant overseer), Mr T Longcl;n (borough sur- veyo:), Mrs Hewitt, Mr Hewitt. ' jun. and Messrs Spencer Wallhead, H Hatton, 11 J Westbrook, 9 Chandley, A Sutton, J Mainwaring, Seeker, Sewards, W Skelton, C D Parkinspn, H Wainwright, T Silk, Wilson, J L Tuostall, and Sidney Wellhead. Mr Lloyd said toe arbitration was taken under the _ _ OPENING OF THE CLAIMAMT'S CASE. _

Lands Clauses Act, which was incorporated in the Warrington Corporation Act, 1899, section three. Section 41 of the same Act empowered the Corporation to acquire property for the widening of Bridge-street from Rylands:street to Market-gate on the west side, and from Market-gate to Holy Trinity Church on the south side. The property his client (Mr Thomas Hewitt) owned was situated in Bridge-street and con- sisted of a blc:ck of freehold property, three shops numbered 44, 46 and 48, and behind them a fully licensed inn, known as the Eagle and Child, having approaches to it by I Patten-lane,—a footpath from Sankey-street and a private road from Rylands-street. The last named approach was made apparently in recent times by his client's predecessor, and at the top of the yard there was a gate which until quite recently had a board upon it stating that the yaral was private to the hotel. On the right hand side of the gable there was a similar notice which was there at the present time. Immediately on the east elle of the passage or road from Rylands-street there was a narrow strip of property belonging to his client, and in whatever leases he had granted there he had always reserved that piece of land obviously with a view of making a wider and batter approach to the back part of the premises in question. In connection with the ion there was a yard and con- siderable outbuildings. There were some sheds, ware- houses and stables. The w hole property according to their measurement was 2,098 yards. He was told they had not exactly agreed with the other side upon the measurement. Their measurement included up to the centre of Patten-lane. There could be no question that they owned the soil up to that point. Toe other side had not included that. At the same time he must point out that it was not in the notice to treat. Ile was told that that accounted for 58 yards of the 70 yards differ- ence between them. Warrington, Mr Lloyd went on to say, was an important town. It had good residential districts and excellent railway, canal and river facilities Its industries were large, important and varied, and being varied,the town didtot suffer from ups and downs in the same way that a one-iodustried town did. It was a prosperous town, and one had only to look at the history of the Corporation's doings to see that. As a progressive Corporation they were providing for the requirements of the borough, and from their expenditure in recent times it was quite clear that the town was increasing as a very pros. pecans town. The area of the eborough in 1891 was 2,004 acres. In 1901 it was 3,115 acres. In 1891 the population was 55,000- In 1901 is was 64.000. In 1891 the rateable value was £166,500 and in 1891 £238,850, Those figures showed what be had ventured to suggest as to the prosperity of the town. To come to Bridge- street. He was not going to enter upon an acrimonious discussion as to whether Bridge-street or Sankey-Street was the better street for business purposes. He was content to say that they were both excellent. Market gate was the centre of the business town, and whether they were within a reasonable distance of it in Sankey- street or in Bridge-street they had excellent business premises. Bridge-strest bad many advantages whatever Sankey-street had, It was the chief access from the County of Chester to Warrington. It was on tne Ches- hire side of the town and favourable for residential pur- poses. It joined a bridge on which ':hree main roads converged. They were important roads, as was illus- trated by the fact that the Northwich-road had already been widened and the Knutsford-road was being widened, while the Corporation had recently decided to widen the bridge itself. That showed that the requirements of the traffic on two, at any rate, of those roads demanded further accommodation and was getting it, and the bridge also required further accommodation and was getting it. Shortly, the traffic was increasing enormously. Tke traffic over the bridge passed mainly uoßridge-street. Part went towards the London and North-Western Railway Stations, but unquestionably the main part came up to the centre of the town along Bridge-street, and so past his client's premises. The street was also similarly used by the people who came in their carriages from the residential parts, while the 'buses from Latchford and Stockton Heath had their termini close to his client's premises. Again the Cor- poration, seeing the state of things he had described were providing for it by embarking upon a system of electrical trams and, recognizing the best place to begin, were going to have the trams along Bridge-street. The trams would run from Bridge-street along Rylands- street, a very short distance from his client's property. The Light Railway Commissioners had authorised a Light Railway to be constructed from Narthwich to Warrington. That traffic would pass over here— Mr Sutton : When it is made. (Laughter.) Mr Lloyd: If it is not made I will not stop to dis- cuss where it will come. But when it is made it will bring furthur traffic over the Corporation lines. The Corporation had for some considerable time been endeavouring to cope with the very largely increasing traffic of Bridge-street. They bad seen it must be an expensive process and had not pone at it in a wholesale fashion. They commenced in 1873 when they obtained a Provisional Order to acquire land for the 4 purpose of widening the west side of the street from the bridge to Friar's-gate. In 1895 they ontained another Pro- visional Order for the purpose of wider-lag the street to 1-Viands-street. The whole of that widening had been done Ls 1899 they went upon the same policy of providing for the increase:of traffic,and obtained the At under which these proceedings were taking place. This showed that the Corporation had long since recognized, that this was one of the most important thoroughfares in the town and one which it was essential to widen at the earliest possible moment. The premises of his client consisted of three shops hiving a frontage in the street. They were old shops but were exceedingly valuable tor the purposes for which they were used Number 44, the one nearest to Market Gate was leased by his client to Messrs Johnson, Bros., the well-known dyers. The lease was dated 24th August, 1898, and granted a term from 25th December. 1898 to September 29th, 1906. The rent reservedifor the first four years was £llO and for the remainder of the term £l2O, the lessee paying rates and taxes, repairing, painting, Szo. Part of these premises were sub-let to Messrs Hodgkinson, Limited. Nos 46apd 48 were both leased to Messrs Hodokinson LimirEd, under a lease, dated August 22 ad, 1896,and running from September 29,1896 for ten years. I should say it is in fair repair and it is old. Mr Sutton: What about the club room and the stairs ? Witness : They are not in good repair. Mr Sutton : Don't you think it wants a good deal more than repairing to put it in anything like decent condition.

Witness : It wants repairing and doing through; perhaps a little more than tnat, but not more than a firm of brewers would do to get the property on lease. He could not say that, if the premises were continued as they were, the licence would not be renewed. The land was now back land but it could be used as front laud' He estimated the value of the lioence at £l,OOO although he was not able to state what the likings were.

THE CASE FOR THE CORPORATION. EVIDENCE CALLED.

In the afternoon the case for the Corporation was opened_ Mr T T Wainwright, valuer of Liverpool was the first witness called. He saki he had had great ex- perience in the valuation of property in Warrington. He had had to survey and value property in Bridge- street, and was well acquainted with prices realized by sales ia Bridge-street, Sankey-street, Horsemarket- street, and Buttermarket-street. Re had made a carefu survey of the premises in question, The buildings were obsolete in character from one end to the other with the exception of the ground floor of the shops and the show- rooms above the front premises. They were old, and although in working order and not delapidated, were.in hit opinion, much worn, if not nearly worn out. The upper storey of the shop where the workwomen were, and which did not appear to have had the attention paid to it in the way of renewals and restorations that the lower storeys had had, was positively unfit for the purposes for which it was used. The rooms were not fit for work rooms for any persons to be in, on account of their delapidated state, lowness of the rooras and the genetal defects in the building. With regard to

the public house, the public portion on the ground floor, had been kept, as far as it was possible to seep an old building, in decent order. With regard to toe upper floor it was so delapidated that he weuld:not be at all surprised if it .came down at any time. The staircase leading to it was unsafe. The only other building which bad " any life in it of any value" was the one at the back used by Mr Wadelin. With regard to the business done at the "Eagle and Child," he bad made full in- quiries and could give the court the result of them if required, but certainly during the last six months. whatever it had been before, it bad been a decreasing business. To tell the truth, he did not know how the tenant had paid the rent, let alone made a living out of the business. The rent paid by the tenant was £6O per annum. He would not be at all surprised if toe doing away with the cattle market was not one reason for the lessening of the trade during the last six or nine months.

MORE ABOUT TBE " EAGLE AND CHILD." EVIDENCE BY THE FORMER LANDLADY.

Mrs Loy, landlady of the Cooper's Arms,aod formerly tenant of the "Eagle and Cnild" hotel now gave evidence. She informed the Court that sbe held the tenancy of the house to; fifteen years. She could not tell them about the profits, but it was a house that never paid her. She paid £65 a year rent. Towards the end of the tenancy thee was a falling off in the business. The yard was not worth as much now as it was in years gone by. Cattle sales used to be held there and since they ceased there was a falling off in business of £3 or £4 per week. Before the cattle sales were done away with the tailings were lost wsen £l5 and £l6 per week, whilst at the present time the takings were about £l2. She had sold as much as three barrels of beer per week but she did not think the sale averaged two now. She did not think the spirit ;rade was any better than the beer trade. Witt reference to the house itself, there was very little accommodation. With regard to its condi- tion, she could assure them that it was most dangerous. A servant fell through from one of the bedrooms into the kitchen on one occasion, and on another, the bed- room ceiling came in and nearly killed herself and her son. She had called the attention of the brewers to the matter, She did not think the takings for the use of the yard had been more than 6s a week ; and she had to pay a man 103 a week to keep the house and yard tidy. The turnover from the sale of mineral waters was about 7s per week. Duriog the whole of the 15 years only two of the stables had been let, and none were let at the present time.. _

In answer to Mr Lloyd, witness said the takings from the public nouse simply kept her. Mr Lloyd : It has kept you fairly comfortably ? Witness : Do you think so? Mr Lloyd : Do you agree ? Witness : I decline to answer you. Mr Lloyd : You have had two boys at the Warring— ton Grammar School? Witness : For a short period. _ .

Mrs Loy again said the' house E imply kept her. Mr Lloyd : It depends on how you want to be kept. Some ladies are kept expensively and some are economi- cal. You have got money in the bank t3-day ? Witness: I shall not answer you. tkir Lloyd : I suggest you have a good sum fof money invested.

Witness : I suggest you are mistaken.: Mr Lloyd : Is it not a fact? Witness : No it is not. Mr Lloyd: flow much money have you in the bank? Witness : I shall not tell you. That is a very impertinent question for you to ask roe. Mr Lloyd : We suggest that you have not only lived in the house for fifteen years, and lived comfortably, but that you have saved money there. Do you say that is not true ?

Witness : I have not saved money there. I lived there for 15 years in the hopes of getting a better house.

Mr Lloyd : Very likely ; some people are never satisfied. Have you lost money ? ' Witness : I have not lost money. Mr Lloyd : You have not saved money? Witness : No.

Further examined, Mrs Loy said she had never kept books. She used to keep a little book in which she entered the day's taking, but the trade became so bad that she got disgusted and gave it up four or five years ago. She went to the Cooper's Arms on the let August but not because Messrs Greensll and Company told her they were giving the Eagle and Child Hotel up. She went simply to better her position. She had heard that the "Eagle and Child" would be given up, but she had asked for a house long before she knew that. She never asked for a reduction of rent. She had never appealed against the assessment of the house but she knew that her kssessmeo t was higher than the rent she paid. Mr Lloyd quoted from a statem.,nt supplied by Mrs Loy, showing that in 1900 she sold from 120 to 12a barrels of beer, but that was one of the best years she ever had. In the present year up to June, 102 gallons of spirits had been cold. In 1900 the sale of spirits amounted to 230 gallons. . .

Mr Wainwright-continued his evidence and said he should never think of adding to the valuation of the " Eagle and child" any sum whatever for the licence of the house in its present conditions. The building was completely worn out. Taking into conaideration the tact that the takings were now £l2 a week, and at the best of times did not exceed £l5, and that all passible chances of making more stables or workrooma were gone, he should say the rate- able value of the place was £BO a year net. His valua- tion of the house was £l,BOO, and his valuation of the whole premises, with 10 per cent, for compulsory sale. was £10,771.

At this point the Court adjourned until Thursday morning.

ST JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH, IURCORN. Lecture by Mr F J Norman. A lecture was given on Wednesday evening last on " Rome" by Mr F J Norman, Mr Pritchard being in the chair. After the introductory remarks the lecturer reminded them' that the lecture was the result of a holiday spent with his wife, and which he earnestly entreated those present to emulate in seeing Rome for themselves. Quoting Austin's(Alfred)eulogy upon Rome, he endorsed the beautiful thoughts—thoughts which were—be was inclined to think superior to those of his poetic works. The lecturer took his audience through the principal scenes connected aa ith St Paul's pilgrimage in thxt City—beautifully describing and illustrating the prison which without doubt is regarded as being his sojourn during his incarceration. At this stage, there was thrown upon the screen a map showing the environment of Rome, and illustrating the various places which gave a g eater inskeht to the subject. Mr. Norman whose knowledge of Roman history appeared to be unlimited enhanced the scenes by graphically describing each view in detail and especially the Forum, where Appius Claudius and the Council of tea invariably met to settle and discuss matters relevant to the State. The Temple of Partheon was one which evinced much interest. Both exterior and interior views were exhibited. Mr Norman who with his wife had visited this building, referred to the symmetry of the work which at first glance scarcely appeared more than of ordinary dimensions until closely noticed, so uniform is the mechanism of its entirety. Raman roads were also dealt vritb, in detail. Mr Norman reminded his hearers that portions of Roman roads were not altogether things of tbe past in this country, for example, he thought the road near which his own house was situated was not of a dis- similar character. (Laughter.) The Catacombs were also a matter of great interest and gave much food for thought, especially in considering the claims for cremation.—The lecture proved altogether most instruc_ tive, and at the close Mr Norman was accorded a vot of thanks.—The Chairman, eulogised upon the brillie ancy of the scenes, Mr Sydney Morrows in seconding said Mr Norman was Inot only a lecturer, but he was also a theologian, and be fas a min- ister felt he was ;almost of themselves. They were doubly indebted to Mr Norman for:the entire cost of printing and of the oxy-hydrogen apparatus were to be met by the Lacturer himself, which amply proved that Mr Norman was heart and seal interested in the object for which the lecture had been promoted.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

MEAN THEFTS ET • WOMAN—CLEVER DECEPTION. On Friday morning two charges of theft against a woman named Elizabeth Ann Byatt, of Liverpool, were investigated at the Warrington County Borough Police Court. In the first ease, prisoner was charged with stealing trora the dwelling house of Samuel Ball, 91, Padgate.lane, two silk blouses, a black dress skirt, • cape, necktie and lady's watch of the total value of about £2 sometime between August 28th and September 7:II—A second charge was that of stealing from the house of Peter Hill, plumber, 105, Padgate-lane, a silk dress skirt and other articles of wearing apparel value £2--- The Chef Constable said the prisoner had been known to Mrs Wilt for some time. it five o'clock on Wednesday afternoon August 28th, Mrs Ball received a telegram from the woman stating that she and her husband were coming to Warrington by the 8.30 train. Mrs Ball sent her daughter to meet them, and when they arrived at the house prisoner said she was going to stay in Warrington for a few elays,previous to taking over her -mother's business in Liverpool. Prisoner and her husband slept at Mrs Bales house that night, but as this arrangement was rather inconvenient, it was subsequently arranged that they should sleep at the house of Mrs Hill, 105, Padgate-lane during their stay in Warrington. They, however, had their food at Mrs Balls, and had the full run of the dwelling house. On September 7th, prisoner went out, saying she was going to the Post Office, and sho never returned. Later in the day, a purse containing 93 q belonging to Mrs Ball's daughter was missed, and this aroused suspicion. 03 examination of the premises the articles detailed in the first charge were missed. Subsequently an inspection of Mrs Hill' house was made, and it was discovered that the articles named in the second charge had disappeared. The prisoner and her husband bad absconded and information was given to the police. They :lad been put to a very great deal of trouble to effect the woman's arrest. She was wanted at various places, including Liverpool, and the Liver- pool police apprehended her some days ago, and com- municated the tact to Warrington. Prisoner and her husband were trieJ at Liverpool on Thursday, and the man was sent to prison for three months. The woman was handed over to the Warrington police. Priseuer said she was acting under the influence of her husband but he (the Chief Constable) would leave it entirely to the Bench to decide whether it was in fear of her husband that she committed these acts of theft and clever deception. Prisoner pawned most of the stolen articles in Warrington and when arrested she was wearing the cape and orie or two more of the missing articles. She was" wanted " at Wigan and other places. It appeared that she hoodwinked the pawnbrokers by giving false names and addresses.— Evidence bearing out this statement was given from which it appeared that the prisoner pawned most of the property at the shops of Messrs G H and H M Jackson, and Mr E V Morton, giving the names of Baker and Pakin.-9aperinten dent Monaghan said that when the ' woman was charged she replied "He (meaning her husband) made me take them."—Prisoner said it was all through her husband that she was in the court. Be had made her de things Since she had been married which she never cid before. He had stolen things at Liverpool and given them to her to pawn, and told her not to use hi 3 name.—The Chief Constable said prisoner was no novice at this kind of thing, but the plea she had just made seemed to have served her in good stead more than once. At Liverpool she had been charged with theft and bound over under the First Offenders Act.—The magistrates committed prisoner to gaol for feur menthe.

SELLING UNWEIGIIED BR D.-WAHRINGTON DEALERS

SUAIITLY FINED.

A batch of prosecutions against provision dealers, for selling unweighed bread, came before the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday. In the first case Messrs Kay and Cowdell, 163, Battersby-laoe,were summoned for selling a loaf of bread without first weighing it, on October lst.—Mr Stanley Wilson,deputy town clerk, prosecuted and said that on the date named a man named Joseph Davies, acting on the instructions of Inspector Place' visited the defendant's shop and aaked for a 21b loaf. He was served with a loaf by one of the shopmen,and it was banded to Mr Wallingtomassist- ant market inspector,who weighed it and found it to be one ounce and four drachms short of 2ltis.The loaf ought to have been weighed in the presence of the purchaser, and that was the real offence.—Mr Cowdell said that Davies simply asked for a twopenny loaf and not a 21b one. He really received a 41 loaf for 21.—The firm were not bakers by trade, but simnly sellers. They took the purchaser of the loaf to be " a tramp on the road."—The Chairman (Mr Monks) We are very glad this case has been brought into Court, bezauso this is one of those instances which lends itself to the general public being defrauded be' those who take advantage of the laxity of inspection- However, we think the justice of the case will be met by fining the defendants 103 and costs.—Jamea Harding, 12, Dudley-street, was sum- moned for a similar offence—Mr Wilson said that the loaf was purchased by the man Davies and found to be an ounce short.—Defendant was fined 10; and coste.— James Leigh, Pinners-brow, was summoned for a like offence.—Mr Wilson said the loaf which should have weigh.d 21b, was four ounces and six drachms short.— The defendant's wife pleaded"ignorance of the law,"and added that she did not profess to sell a 2lb loaf for 21, the price paid by the purchaser.—Mr Wilson : The authorities have had strong complaints about people selling bread without weighing, it. The Chairman : There is an unlimited amount of fraud.—D2fendant was told by the Chairman that she "would have to pay for her ignorance"and she was fined 20s. and costs.—Ed ward Smith, 33, Fennel-street, was charged with a like offence. It was proved that the loaf was two ounces short, and the defendant was fined 10S, and costi3.—Anchew Templeton, 11, Irlam-street, pleaded guilty to a similar offence, and Mr Wilson stated that the loaf was 2,iz, and 121 s dracbms short of 211). Defendant said he had given strict orders to his wife to weigh all bread. They had only been in the shop six months, and his wife bad neglected co weigh the loaf with not being accustomed to it.—The Chai-man : If you give instruotions it is not for us to see that they are carried out. If they are not, you are liable. Yu will be fined 103 and costs.—Defendant : I will mind in the future.—The Chairman : You will pay 10s. and costs for the past.

WOMAN VIOLENTLY A°3AELTED AT WAREIVOTON. At the Warringson Police Court on Monday, before Mr F Monks and other magistrates, a young man named Christopher Ayres, 9, Antrobus-street, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in that street,at twenty minutes past five on Sunday afternoon and, further, with assaulting Jane Eaves and Mary Straw at the same time and place. , Mary Straw Winwick-road, who appeared in the witness box with a badly discoloured eye, said that on Sunday afternoon prisoner was in Antrobus-street under the influence of drink, and "wanted to fight anyone." Witness went up to see what was the matter, and prisoner struck her three severe blows in the eye with his fist.—Jane Eaves, 68 years of age, and mother of the last witness, who also bore signs of illusage, said she saw the prisoner strike her daughter in the face, and she told him to stop. Before she had got the words out of her mouth, he struck her a blow in the face, which knocked her to the ground. The man was a complete stranger to her, and she bad never spoken to him previously.—

P.C. Lewis said his attention was called to the prisoner who was surrounded by a large crowd of people, and was drunk and making use of bad language. He took him into custody. and witness was a the opinion that it was fortunate for the prisoner that he did so, or the people would have thrashed him.—Prisoner now expressed his sorrow and said that had he been sober he would not have committed the assaults. Five or six men struck and kicked him.—The Chairman said the magistrates were sorry they could not allow the ladies any compensation under the law. It was one of the worst cases of aggravated assault which be had ever dealt with. Prisoner would be committed to gaol for two months with hard labour—Ultimately it was decided to allow the female witnesses 103 each from the poor box. DISGRACEFUL CONDLICT NEAR WARRINGTON.

THE NORTHERN LEAGUE. Warrington Beaten at Leigh.

Warrington did not journey to Leigh with very much confidence on Saturday, for the team was not at full strength and it was no secret that the Mather-laners were bent on doing what they were unable to do in the West Lancasbirecup final, i.e. beat Warrington. The visitors were short of three regular forwards in J Swift, Scholtze, and Edmondson, substitutes being found in Pemberton, F Swift and Edwards. Hockenhall in place of Burgess at half back wasalso an experiment forced on the club by the latter's suspension. Leigh were at full strength, and though the weather was wet and stormy a good crowd assembled. Several hundred spectators came from Warrington by special train. Mr J Priestley I was the referee and the teams lined out as follow. Leigh : E Pearson, full back; Ganley, Right, John- soh and T Davies, three-quarters ; Dunbavin and aloly- neux, half backs; Woodward, Boardman, W Roberts, J Roberts, Ecclaston, Ramsdale, Webster and Clare, for- wards. F Swift, Cunningham, Fell, Pemberton„ Warrington : Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, three-quarters ; Boardman Warrington won the toss, and started with a strong ManodrrEi. wind behindperri, Molyneux setting the ball in motion. donv:aErdlse,nforwards. Naturally Leigh were soon called up m to defend, which they did with great determination and good tactics. Warrington kept in the home half, but made little pro- gress towards a score, though the Leigh line had some lucky escapes. Dunbavin's kick was charged once, but the Warrington forwards bungled the chance. Then Pearson mulled near the goal, only for Warrington to be robbed cleverly by Johnson. Dicken- son punted down and following up well upset Pearson a hot attack on the home line following. Hallam tried at goal from a penalty but the shot passed outside. War- rington pressed and Fish went half back, immediately sending a long pass to Isherwood who chose to struggle for the line himself instead of giving Hockenhall an easy try at corner. The Leigh men easily accounted for Isherwood and Fish went back to the wing as the line was cleared.Warrington were beaten in the scrimmages, but got the ball occasionally. Fish made two_ brilliant runs down touch but failed t 3 score and balft time was not far distant when Warrington again go close in. Hockenhall had a grand chance given him by Allen but missed the pass Leigh dribbled into the Warrington half beaded by Ramsdale, Fish and Dicken- son carrying play back again by clever combination. Warrington passed across and Isherwood let another chance go begging. The attack was maintained and Dickenson giving Harris a long pass enabled the wing man to score far out. Hallam failed at goal and just before the interval Boardman was carried off with an injured knee. Half-time, Warrington 1 try, Leigh nil. Eden restarted and Leigh soon got the upper hand, Dunbavin making a poor shot at goal from a penalty in the first five minutes. Playing a man short Warrington had little chance of keeping the home team at bay, and very soon Molyneux credited his side with a dropped goal. Warrington responded with a dashing dribble to the Leigh half, where Pearson was brought down. A penalty further improved matters and Harris put in a useful sprint, Leigh having to defend. A long kick

===== 0002601_19011011_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
Hull v. Halifax.

These clubs met at Hull, before 10,000 spectators. Hull scored just before half-lime. After the interval it was evident that it had been a punishing game, and Hull tired, enabling Nicholson to score. Hull now made ground, and strong play against the backs gave To vns- end a chance. Morley and Riggs brought relief, and maintained a strong attack in the Hall territory, but the defence was sound, and the game fluctuated at the centre until the close, when .the scores stood :—Hull, two tries (six points) ; Halifax, one try (three points).

===== 0002601_19011011_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
Salfora v. Bradford.

1 This match was played at New Barns in boisterous weather, before 5,000 spectators. Rain fell heavily and socklened the ground. This suited the heavy Bradford forwards, who carried several scrummages and gave their backs plenty of opportunities. Heseltine and Sinton madej good use of them, and each scored a try for Bradford,who led at half time by six points to two. Although playing against the wind. Bradford com- menced the second half in vigorous fashion, and Hodg- son scored a try which Marsden improved. Salford then appeared to tire, and their passing was far too slow to be effective. Heseltine intercepted a pass from Williams to Bone,and after a fine run managed to place the bail over the Saltord line and score another unimproved try for Bradford. These reverses stimulated the home team, and from this point to the end there was a decided improvement in their play. Lomas dodged all the Bradford hacks and scored a try for Salford,from which he kicked a goal, and Williams further reduced Brad- ford's lead by scoring another try. The home team kept the visitors on the defence until the finish, and the game ended Bradford,one goal and four tries (14 points); Salford, two goals and two tries (10 points).

LANCASHIRE RUGBY COMBINA-

TION.

A Victory for Fletcher Russell's.

The visit of Oldham "A " team to the Lovely-lane enclosure, aroused considerable interest,and despite the unpropitious weather, there was a large assembly of spectators to witness the match. Two alterations had been made in the home team. Holbrook and Peter Knight displacing Potter and hill. The Oldham team was representative, Wild and Mort taking the places of Booser and Bush. Broom kicked off for the visitors, who immediately forced play to the home half. Hostili- ties ceased fur a short time, the result of an accident to Earnsbaw. On re-starting, the home backs took play to the Oldham end, several dangerous scrimmages taking place on the line. A mark by the Oldham back relieved the pressure, and play was for a time confined to the centre. Smart work by the locale saw Clare get possession, and after a tricky ran that player passed to Simmins, who scored a grand try at the corner. The goal kick failed. Following this reverse the visitors smartened up scmewhat,and several pangerous rushes were frustrated by Brown, the home full back. Smart work behind the pack saw Oldham get dossession, an excellent passing movement being spoiled by a throw forward. Half-time arrived with the score:— Fletcher's, one try; Oldham,nil. On resuming the visitors tried hard to equahz,buthe home defence was safe. Ihe home team gradually worked their way to the centre, and play ruled even for a time. From a scrimmage Spottiewoode gained possession, and after a grand run, was pulled up on the line. Play still continued danger- ously near the home end, and from a free kick, Dick- son kicked a fine goal. No further scoring resulted,and /the game ended ; Fletcher Russell's, one try (three points) ; Olddam, one goal (two points).

Runcorn Petty Sessions.

MONDAY. —Before Messrs GL Davies and R H Poanett.

TEMPORARY AUTRORITy.--dernee Bradley applied for a transfer of licence from Joseph Blackburn for the " Three Crowns Inn." a beerhouse in King—street. Superintendent Ennion said be was quite satisfied with the application which was accordingly gianted.—Mlchael fileGeachin was granted permission to sell at the beerhouse "The Friendship Tavern" In Friar-street and a bagatelle licence was also granted for the house.

SMART PENALTY YoR WANTON DAMAGE.—John Bowden, 22, Taylor's-row. was summoned by Mary Baktup, also of Taylor's-row, for committing damage to the value of 10s to a pair of scales, jugs and plates on the 7th beptember.— Prosecutnx said that on the date named at 8 o'clock pm, prisoner came into her shop at Shalicross-row and used threatening language towards her. its afterwards struck her on the breast. kle then pulled the counter over and broke the things mentioned. He was not sober.—Defendant, who denied the assault, but admitted breaking the articles, was fined 204 and 8s 6d costs, also the cost of the damage 10s. FOUR Mishmkvous BovB.—Pour small boys named Joseph Flannagan, aged 12, David Moores, 13, Joseph Moores, 10, and John Handley, 11, were summoned for throwing stones. —P C Wilding stated that on the 22nd September he was in Cooper-street when he saw the defendants throwing at some property. There had been many Complaints about stone throwing, sad a considerable amount of damage had been done.—Defendants were each fined 2s 6d aud costs, or la default 7 days. The money was paid.

WARRINGTON UNION ASSESS- MENT COMMITTEE;

A Small Increase in Rateable Value. A meeting of the above mentioned committee was held in the Board room at the Woikhouse on Friday,Mr G Pilling presiding. There were also present :—Messrs A J Jolley, A Jolley, William Houghton, J Billinge, T Fialton, J Clarke, It Forshaw with .1 C Sutton (clerk), S Sutton (assistant clerk), William Owen (valuer).

===== 0002601_19011011_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RATEABLE VALUE OF THE BOROUGH

The CLERK presented an inform al statement of the valuation of the Borough. He stated that the full figures would be submitted at the next meeting. The present assessable value of the borough was .£239,500 as compared with £237,909 in the previous half-year, showing an increase of £1,591: The Clerk pointed out that the increased value was not in the same ratio as the? had experienced in previous half years. In answer to Mr BILLINGE, the CLERK said he did not think the rate of increase would be anything like in the future what they had been experiencing for the past few years unless trade and manufactories were very prosperous.

Mr BILLINGE : The Increase this half-year is largely due to new property? The Cr-midc said that was so, but the rate of increase in new property had not been very great during the past half-year. Mr WOODCOCK said it was one of the slightest half- years he could remember. He also pointed oat that in the appeals before the Committee they had only lost L'ls6 las in rateable value. This was considered satisfactory. The statement was adopted on the proposition of Mr Jolley.

===== 0002601_19011011_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
V.ALL'E WS REPORT.

Mr OWES (valuer), submitted his monthly report of valuations and inspections in the township of Warring- I tom Mr Owen said he had surveyed the new buildings of the Langford Wire Co. Ltd, which were almost com- pleted. They were in partial occupation for experimental purposes only, and not for manufacturing: The case would be met by a temporary six months assessment of gross £75, rateable £57. The sing and Queen Inn. Pad- gate. flats house was now completed and occupied, gross £lOO, rateab'e £B5. Messrs Bishop and Co. Ltd. Works and Office, Dial Yard. Gross £2lO 103, rateable £l6B. This assessment was temporarily reduced to gross £lO5, rateable £B4. In the township of Newton-le-Willows--Mr Alex Brown, new house, Crow-lane, gross £37 10, rateable £33 10. The Earlestown Co-operative Society Limited, stabling,' stores and yard, School Street, Cross Lane, Earlestown, gross £6O, rateable £5l ; house adjoining, gross £l3, rateable £ll ; new shop, Earl Street, Earles town, gross £35, rateable £3O : house adjoining, gross £l5, rateable £l3; Messrs R Walton and Company,flint glass works, gross £63, rateable £5O. An appeal was made at the last meeting on the ground that the manufacture of glass had ceased altogether, and it was not to be resumed by the present owner. The works were occupied in finishing the stock of glass goods already made, and the engine and boiler were in use. Say temporary assessment of gross £45, rateable £36.

In the township of Rixton, Mr Adam Robertson, Rixton New Hall Farm, decreased to gross £422 103 ; rateable, £387. In the township of Croft Mr Owen reported that he was asked by the committee to look at some of the cottages in Croft which had low assessments. Ile found there were altogether about 125 cottages fin the town ship, and all except one were let at less then 33 61 a week. There were nine cottages cow empty, six of which were cottages which bad boen improved by their owners and for which 33 61 and 4s a week each was being asked and could not be obtained, and the other three were condemned. Some of the cottages were let as low as 1s 93 • week, but on visiting them he was. satisfied that the rent was sufficient for the accommo- dation and Condition.—Aaron Marsh, Deakin's cottage and stable, and 22 perches garden, Smith's Lane. Now £6 53 gross, rateable £5, should be raised to £ll Gross, £9 103 rateable. {The stables had been increased. Charles Stanly Smith, Heath House, house, stables, outbuildings and gardens ai acres, gross £5O, rateable £5l ; two cottages, each £5 gross,£4 rateable,occupied by own servants. The house and stabling were now occupied, and the cottages, which were converted out of an old house, would be occupied in a few days. The " Hermit Inn."—Tne present assemiment-7ros9 £26, rateable £22 —should be increased to gross £35, rateable £3O. Tea room had been increased in size.

The appeals of the Manchester Ship Canal Company on premises in Wrrrington. and of Richard Evans and Co. on mines and royalties in flaydoett were still under con.ideration by the Clerk and himself. The report was adopted.

HOCKEY.

Stockton Heath v Oxton.

Strickton Heath played their second match of the season against Oxton at Oxton, Liverpool, on Saturday, They turned up with only ten men but Oxton kindly found them a substitute. The ground was hi splendid condition and a close and interesting game ensued. Stockton Heath were the first to score by means of Gandy, Oxton Equaliz;ng shortly afterwards. Half- time 1 all. In the second half Bond and Haitian Owen scored for Stockton Heath, Oxton scoring 1 goal, although a hot shot from the outside right was only inches short of equalizing. Final score : Stockton Heath, 3 goals ; Oxton, 2.

WARRINGTON. Foreign Missions. At the monthly devotional meeting of the Bold- street Wesley Guild held on Monday evening, the topic for discussion was "Foreign Missions," and short addresses were given by the Rev T Bishop and Mr Littler. The meeting concluded with short prayers by several members.

WARRINGTON.

Licensing Transfers. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, the following licensing transfers were granted : Sceptre Beerhouse, Winwick-road, from Elizabeth Bentley to Thomas Shaw; Royal Exchange, Golden- square, from Jane It Balton to Thomas B Malkia; off-licence, 63, Priestly-street, from W S Beecham to Norah Freeth off-licence, 33. Pinner's-brow, from John Flynn to Mary Jane Williams. Firewood Manutactory Burnt Down. At about half-past four on Sunday afternoon two men named Rylance and Hands, living in Glass- house-row, noticed that the premises in Factory- yard used as a firewood manufactory were in flames Messrs Crosfield's fire brigade was sent for, and some of the members soon put in an appearance,and commenced to play on the burning building,but the fire bad obtained a firm hold, and the manufactory wes completely gutted. Long Service and Good Conduct Medals. The War Office base published a list of non-com: rcissioned officers and men who have been awarded medals for long service and good conduct. Included are the following :—South Lancashire Regiment: 334, Colour-Sergeant E O'Brien, Sergeant C Ings.— Cheshire Regiment: 149, Sergeant F A Rallis, 83 Colour-Sergeant ji Facer.

The Field Club:

The winter sessiOn.v;s:;.o"pe;;;;;;.n Friday evening Then Mr Charles White, lectured in the Museum Lecture Room to a good audience, "On the denuda- tion of the Secondary Rocks in the North-Western Highlands of Scotland." After clearly and care- fully defining the contents of his terms " denuda- tion," and" Secondary Rocks,' and pointing out that these latter had, in the region under review, , once exceeded a mile in thickness ; though since reduced to barely a fifth of that; he showed how the denuding action of frosi' wind and water. I would have swept away even that fifth, but for the protective covering of volcanic matter, which welling up through craters and fissures, in early Tertiary times, had overflowed large areas in , Ireland and Scotland. The lecturer endeavoured to I convey to his hearers some real notion of the vast I duration of the secondary period, by estimating the averge rate of denudation, and suggesting that that of aeposition bad been even slower. In the discussion which followed Mr White was cordially congratulated on the skill and success with which he had treated a somewhat technical theme in a manner" to be understanded of the people' Ths ee was a good one, and the club enters upon imtslt7itnhgwinter with unimpaired energy. The pro- gramme is varied and quite up to the high standard this society has maintained since its foundation.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS. =— Martial Law in Cape Colony,

A Lost Gun Recaptured.

Lord Kitchener reports the recapture of the seven- pounder recently taken from the South African Con stabulary near Routkop. Pursuing his retreat northwards, it is officially announced that Botha has crossed the Pivaan River Foiled in his projected invasion of Natal, the Boor Com- mandant-General has cDntrived to escape from the cordon of our troops. Martial law has been proclaimed at Capetown through out Cape Colony. The authorities at Capetown have been divided on the subject, but those opposed have bad to yield to the exigencies of the military situation. and to the urgent necessity of getting at the head cen- tres of sedition in the colony. Martial law will be enforced with all possible leniency, and business affairs will not be interfered with. That there will be a vig orous exerci3e of the censorship is not open to doubt.

Botha Hotly Pursued.

A message of Wednesday from Durban says that Botha is being hotly pursued by Bruce-Hamilton. General Hamiltcn has had several engagements with the flying Boers.

South Lancashire Losses in Zululand. The War Office has issued toe list of casualties among non-commissioned officers and men at Fort Itala in Zululand on the 26:h ult. The losses sustained by the 14 South Lancasnire Regiment were as follows :—Killed 5654 Private J Walk Pr. 3299 Private R Golding, 5798 Private R Keating. 3269 Private W Jones, 5544 Private E Pennington, 4470 Private W Ford, and 5073 Private C Crawford ; dangerously wounded, 3257 Private J Jack- son and 3334 Private E Bentman ; severely woundeei 5654 Private F Bradbury. 5537 Private J Fallon, 3107 Private F Clarke, and 3807 Lance-Corparal D Wilson ; slightly wounded, 3195 Corporal C Wintersgill. 3310 Private F Hughes, and 3972 Private CSML Duffy.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Oa Tuesday afternoon the fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse.—Present Messrs J Stoves (in the chair), A R Norman, J P (vice-chair- man), T Wood, J Douglas, the members of the Rural Council with Messrs G F Ashton (clerk), W E Bough (deputy clerk) and J Cunnah (master.) TENDERS.

Mr Linaker said that he had given notice at the last meeting with regard to the course adopted for the con- sideration of tenders. It struck him then that havine only ten minutes to consider the tenders by the iadivi- dual members of the Board it did not seem a business- like method of procedure. No commercial concern would consider the tenders in the way they did, and he did not see how they could arrive at a right decision with the short time at their disposal. On looking at the tenders accepted at the last meeting he found that one of the tenders had been accepted which was really the highest. A committee of 5 7 or 11 to meet say at 11 o'clock in the morning of the day the tenders had to be accepted could report to the Board meeting. Mr Ashton's clerk could also go through the tenders and see of they had been properly worked 'out. Any designing person could put certain figures down and work out the totals at lower figures thereby mak- ing the totals less than they ought to be. He moved that it would be much more satisfactory to appoint a small committee to be called the Contract Committee to examine the tenders.. _ .

'Mr Talbot seconded. Mr Norman asked would it not be well if the com- mittee consisted of the whole of the Board ? Mr Linaker said that a small committee could consider each tender whereas in the whole of the Board there were "two here" "and "two there" considering tenders all asking at once for the assistance of the master. Mr Norman thought it would be as easy to call the whole of the Board as eight or nine members. Mr Woods proposed as an amendment that the House Committee, which consisted of the whole of the Board, be called the Contract Committee and should be asked to meet an hour before the time of the Board to consider the tenders.At the last meeting the method adopted was a very slipshodjaffair altogether owing to the shortness of time. Mr Woodward seconded. There might be occasions on which some particular member of the Board might wish , to have a voice in some of the tenders. He would not then have the same advantage as those who bad been on the committee. Mr Linaker re-affirmed that the tenders should not be Considered in a limited time. They were spending rate- payers' money and they ought to do it to the best ad- vantage. On the amendment being put it was carried only the proposer and seconder voting for the proposition. The Clerk said that the coke tender was deferred at the last meeting. There was only one tender from the Frodsham Gas Company at 153 per ten. It was esti- mated that 50 tons would be required which would be more than £37 10. The matter was again deferred to a later date. A CROWDED HOUSE, It was reported that there were at present 158 inmates in the House against 134 last year which had caused the beds to be occupied. The Chairman asked if the weeding process wag going OD. Mr Mosely Williams replied that it was, and that with regard to the tire-escape, Merryweather's were going on with the work which had been delayed owing to one of their men taking a holiday.

This concluded the raeeing.

A PENSION FOR Mn GARRATT RONA:ENE. After waiting something like 40 years, Mr Garrett Ronayne an Indian Mutiny veterantand an old resident at Earlestown, has at last received a notification from the India Office that he has been granted a pension as from the Ist October this year.
