NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT].

At the monthly meeting of the District Counci this week the members had an opportunity of bearding the Chairman of the Water Committee and getting from him some explanation of the recent failure of the water supply. From what he stated, the inhabitants can again breathe freely as it seems the failure was only of a temporary nature, duerifrom accidental causes, and cannot b 3 any means be taken as indicating that the Council have made a serious blunder in their water scheme. It was the current topic that the undertaking was a failure, but if we are to accept the report of Prof. Boyd Dawkins, as correct then the people are wrong in their theories, and the Council are right. The Professor says" I am of opinion that an ample supply for the future needs of the district may be obtained within he area of the present site of the waterworks." There is some consolation in this, and perhaps the fault lies not with the water, but with the machinery used in obtaining it.

The Chairman of the Gas Committee was also on his defence in connection with the quality of the gas supplied to the neighbourhood, which is really very bad as an illuminant. Mr Emmett brought the question forward and he was backed up by Dr Latham. An object lesson was the illumination of the Council Chamber, and Mr Emmett, said from that they would be able to see what kind of gas they were getting. A walk through the district will convince anyone that the illuminating power of the gas is not what it ought to be, but it seems something is being done to ascertain where the mischief lies. Perhaps when the Light Railway is an accomplished fact in the district, we shall not have any need for the gas.

The Council Chamber has become richer this week by the hanging of the late Colonel McCorquodale's portrait which has been presented to the Council by Mr A C McCorquodale and has been gratefully ac- cepted by the Council. With the exception of Mr Emmett none of the members of the Council sat with Colonel McCorquodale on the old Board of Improvement Commissioners, but Mr Emmett paid an excellent tribute to the deceased gentleman for his tact and peace loving qualities. It is fitting that the portrait of a former chairman of the local governing body should occupy a place of honour in the Council Chamber, and though he may not be present in body, the influence of his photograph I may guide his successors to a right conclusion in the discussion of any knotty points which may arise.

Such a glorious time for organi.its in Earlestown and Newton has not been known before. Before the end of the year, no less than three organists will be required The Congregational Church organ is vacant, the Wesleyan organ will be vacant in about a month hence, and the organ at St. Peter's Church, Newton, will require a new official 1)3. the end of the year.

===== 0002601_19011011_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

Football was at -a--diSC-o-u-nt in Earlestown and District on Saturday, none of the clnbs having a match at home. Earlestown played the first round of the English Cup competition; paying a visit to the new St Helens Town Club. This was the second time Earlestown have met St Helens Town at St Helens, the first occasion being the first round of the Lancashire Senior Cup, when they were defeat- ed by two goals to nil. On Saturday they again received their quietus, and no doubt they will by this time have a lively recollection of the Townites in cup ties. Although defeated by two goals to one Earlestown were decidedly unlucky to lose, the winning goal being scored whilst the backs were clustering round the referee, fruitlessly claiming for offside. Dawson failed to put in an appearance and the Earlestown front rank was considerably disorganised thwreby. The most fitting result of the game would have been a draw, as in the play Earlestown were every bit as good as their oppon- ents. The Town were more dangerous when they got near goal, their forwards never hesitating to stoot when a favourably opportunity presented itself. A regrettable incident occurred during the game, Barlow and Limond having a regular prize fight, the intervention of the referee being occasioned.

Newton will have good reason to remember their visit to Rhyl under the auspices of the Combination this season, as after being all over their opponents right through the game, the Welshmen ran out winners by eight goals to one. This is the sec erest thrashing Newton have received for some time,and the recent performances of the Nursery Park brigade renders it all the harder to understand: To-morrow Chirk pay them a visit and Newton will have to play a vastly improved game if they are to retrieve themselves in the good books of their supporters.

To-morrow Earlestown are again without a match and on Saturday week they play their first home League match, Stalybridge Rovers being their visitors. It is ho ped that their supporters will turn out in large nu mbers to welcome them, as the club has been put to a great expense, playing so many away matches at the commencement of the season.

WARRINGTON. Buckley•street Band of Hope.

• The lint entertainment of the season in connec- tion with the above society was held in the school- room on Monday evening. Mr: J Palmer occupied the chair, and gave a short address. An excellent programme was arranged by Miss E Plinston and Mr A Trow consisticg of solos,recitations &c. Solos were rendered by Mr W Richardson, Miss Lilly Fairhurst and Miss Duckworth. Mr E Taylor gave a cornet solo. Me reciters were : Misses Cross, Horton, E Scott, L Smith, and N Bramhall. Miss Cross recited "Farewell to the bottle" in a splendid manner and Misses Lily Fairhurst and Duckworth were heartily encored. Mr A Trow accompanied at the organ and Miss P Plinston at the piano. Much amusement was created by a dialogue entitled "Mr Spooner 's clients." A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Miss E Plinsten and all who assisted to make the entertainment a success, and a few ap- propriate remarks by Mr A Jennings and Mr R Fogg brought a highly creditable, and encouraging gathering to a close.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, OCTOBER 11, 1901

advise that a well be carried down to a depth of 2101 t., Ithat it be 10ft. in diameter, and that it be connected with the boring by adits, driven trona the bottom of the shafts. If this be carried out the present • yield will be Most probable more than doubled, and amount to more than 500,000 gallons a day. It is improbable that the supply trom this site will be affected by coal mining in the near future if at all. The objection applies more or less to all sites on the Triassic sandstone which are available for the water supply of the district. I see no advantage in abandoning this site in which water has been proved, for another further off, which may be opposed, and may have to be fought through the Houses of Parliament at considerable cost. I am, dear sir, yours truly. W. BOYD DAWKINS." The Chairman said be wanted to express the regret of the committee at the recent breakdown at the water works. It had been an unfortunate breakdown and bad caused great annoyance to the consumers of water in the district The committee and tneir engineer took immediate steps to rectify it and he was pleased to say they had overcome temporarily the difficulty. There were still defects, not only in the machinery, but in the supply, but the committee had passed resolutions to obtain the sanction of the Local Government Board for borrowing money.to complete the scheme and an inquiry would shortly take place. They had also applied tor a large sum to lay new mains so that the distribution of water would be improved. He had to acknowledge in the name of the committee the courtesy of the London and North- Western Railway Company in coming forward in the emergency and supplying the deficiency in the water supply. Mr Gilmour contended that the people who had had the water supply shortened were not given sufficient notice.

Mr Barton said notices were sent cu/*. Since Saturday the whole of the district had been amply supplied.

The minutes were then passed. 4 HIGHWAYS AND SANITARY COMMITTEE.

_ A letter was read from the Clerk of the County Council stating that the Main Roads and Bridges Committee are of opinion that in view of the proposed tramways to be constructed in this district, the com- mittee cannot increase the offer of £650 made to this Council for the ordinary maintenance and repair of the main roads in their district. A letter was read from Mr A. C Leslie (Lord Newton's Estate Agent), stating that ,he would be glad to meet a deputation from this Council to discuss the question of the fencing of Thompson's Pit. it was resolved that the matter be referred to the repre- sentatives of Wargrave Ward and Dr Latham. A letter was read from Mr T Orman& the hon. snore - tary and treasurer of the Win wick Educational Foun- dation, with respect o the tipping of rubbish on land in the occupation of Mr James Berry, Penkford Farm, Newton Common, stating that the Governors will not offer any objection to the Committee using the pit in question for tipping dry rubbish providing the Council will undertake to protect the hedge and ditch which is their boundary on the south side of the disused pit, and put in, or let the tenant put in, at the expense of the Council, such drain pipes as may be required to carry off all the surface water from the land. It was resolved that the thanks of the Committee be given to the Governors of the Winwick EduJational Foundation for 'allowing the pit in question to be used as a tip, and that the requirements contained in Mr Ormand's letter be carried out by the Council. - The Clerk submitted to the Committee his correspon- dence with reference to the pollution of Millingtord , Brook. He also read a letter which be received from Mr C S Bennie, Clerk te the Golborne Urban District ICouncil, on the 23rd ultimo, stating that something bad been done to bring about a very marked change in the ' state of the brook, as he was surprised to tied the stream so clear at the Golborne Mills on the previous day. It was resolved that the Inspector of Nuisances be directed to report to the next meeting of ttois Com- mittee as to the state of the brook.

The Surveyor was also directed to report to the next meeting of this Committee as to the state of SAnkey Brook before it enters this district, and also to report as to the advisability or otherwise of the Council adopting some warn carriage systern,aad also which system is the best adapted for the district. • A circu'ar IFtter was read from the Local Government Board with respect to the Isolation Hospital Act, 1901. It was resolved that the Isolation Hospital Suli•Com- mittee be requested when considering the question of the provision of an Isolation Hospital for the district to consider the advisability of the provision of a joint hospital by this and other districts. A letter was read before the Water Committee from Mr F W Webb, 'superintendent of the locomotive department of the London and North-Western Railway, Crewe, stating that in Consequence of the Wigan Cor- poration having cut off the Company's supply at Wigan the Company would require the whole of their water from Parkside,and that they would not be able to supply the Council with any after tho 15th September inst. The report of the engineer for the past month was read, from which it appeared that the clacks, bucket, of No. 2 borehole had during the night of the 22a,1 inst., got disconnected from the pumping-rod, and that he had ordered Messrs See and Company to at once take the necessary means for putting the borehole into work- ing order. It was resolved that the action of the engineer be approved and adopted. The Chairman reported that he had directed the engineer to at once see Mr F W Webb, at Crewe, with respect to the obtaining of a temporary supply of water by the London and North-Western Rail- way Company and his action was approved. The Clerk submitted to the committee print of the Local Government Boar's provisional orders confirmation (No 2) Act, 1901, confirming the Provisional Order for extending the borrowing powers of the Council under the Newton Improvement Act, 1855, for purposes of water supply. The following report of the proceedings of the meet- ing of the special water committee held on the 6th insh was read and adopted :—That the boxes for containing the specimens of rock, &e, encountered in the boring operations, and at present kept at the Town Hall, be delivered to the ens,ineer at the gasworks. That the engineer obtain a quotation from Messrs It Evans and Co., Ltd., for the supply of cal to the Water Works, at the rate of about 20 tons per week to the end of May, 1902, and that the same be submitted to the next meet- ing of the Water Committee. That the Special Water I Committee be requested to make arrangements for as- certaining the quantity of water pumped daily from each of the two boreholes, and of the quantity supplied to the inhabitants ot the district.

Professor W Boyd Dawkins D.Sc.F.R.S. presented the following report on the water supply. " I beg to send you the results of my examination of the district and of all the evidence which I have been able to collect as to the supply of water on the site of the present water works. The Triassic sandstones of the district are highly charged with water which is gravitating seawards tram the higher to the lower levels. They are of considerable thickness,the lower divisionmenetrated by the bore holes at the waterworks being 504 feet thick. They occupy a very large area, amounting to many square miles, ranging northwards as far as the South Lancashire coalfields, and eastwards in the direction of Manchester. The rocks on the site of the waterworks form part of this area,ranging northwards and eastwards f,3r several square miles. It is impossible to define this exactly because it is impossible to isolate this district from the general area. Tne amount of water percolating into the rock from the rainfall is enormous. One inch of rain over a square mile of surface represents a daily supply of 40,000 gallons, and as in these rocks it is not less than 10 inches, that figure multiplied by ten. The two boreholes at the waterworks yield between them 247,000 gallons per day, and although they are not more than 100 feet apart they do not draw upon one another. When the pumps are at rest t..e water is at 35 feet below the surface, while the pumping level is in No 1 borehole 210 feet from the surface m 95.5 feet, and in No 2 borehole 150 feet. There are several wells in the immediate neighbourhood which draw upon the supply of water in these lower sandstone rocks, of which it Is only necessary to quote 2. One, the London and North Western wells at Park Side, 10 feet in diameter, penetrate these rocks to a depth of 100 feet 6 inches. From this point a borehole is carried down to a depth of 457 feet. The the supply fo rest of the hthte:atNerewisto2nl ,feet from the surface, and the daily yield is about ps6a7r;eorowoorts sao derived alilorlsle dTewov' h ed from the same rocks, and the yield is about 432,000 gallons per day. Both these wells are about half a mile from the site of the) water- works. and have no appreciable effect on their water level. It may be concluded, from the fact that neither of these wells draw on the boreholes at the waterworks that the water stored up in these rocks is not being depleted by excessive pumping, and that the water per- colating into them from the rainfall IS more than suffi- cient to meet the demand made upon them. It may also be inferred that the supply 'alit be permanent from the fact that the exceptional drought has left no mark on the present yield. From the consideration of all these facts lam of opinion that an ample supply for the future needs of the district may be obtained within the area of the prosent site of the waterworks. I would

NOTES ON (JAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovahst."

The team Warrington sent to Leigh on Saturday was thoroughly well beaten by twelve points to three points and if a better fifteen cannot be put in the field when one or two regular player!' happen to be absent then it is quite certain, that amngton will soon be progress- ing (?) towards the bottom a the League. The weakness was most apparent at half back and forward. Everywhere else Warrington can be relied upon to hold their own, but in the first and second lines there is a woeful lack of strength. Of course the absence of such men as J. Swift, Burgess, and Scholtze makes a• differ- ence, but then a club like Warrington is surely expected to have at command decent reserves to fill the places of two forwards and a half back. Unfortun- ately they have not. The forwards on Saturday were, as a set, a disgrace to any Northern League team, while the halves were a long way below class.

I am not alluding to or blaming the men individually. They did as well as could be expected and the fault is not theirs. Take Boardman and Fell out of Saturday's eight—and one of them was idle through injuries half the game—and there is not another man anything like heavy enough or big enough for forward play and not all of them are smart enough by a long chalk.

When Warrington have their full tearn in the field they are practically—and I say it without reserve— playing all the good men the Club possesses. The paucity of reserves is scandalous, and if the committee cares to face facts they will just imagine for a minute the state of the team should Isherwood, Fish, Hallam or any of the backs get disabled. Class backs are diffi- cult to obtain I know as well as anyone and I make full allowance for this fact. Yet they must be got. Of forwards there is no such dearth and it is no credit to the Club that they have so few scrimmagers worthy of playing in Northern League football. They are not grown in Warrington, but they can be imported like Fell and Edmondson—two of the best—were last sea- son. And why have they not been? There was more than one good forward left at Askam who could have been signed on for Warrington and who has since gone to Hull and other places. Even now an enterprising committeeman could, I am sure, pick a quartette of strapping young fellows from some of the Cumberland junior clubs who would train up into really good men. It is not difficult to make a good forward out of a man if he only has the physique. Let us see some evidence of the management's energy before this season's pros- peers are blighted,

Of late I have not said much in the way of criticism, though like the parrot I have "thought a lot." I have not even seen the "A " team play this season, but good judges tell me there is not a good forward in the whole bunch doing duty, and I know very well that, bar Ratcliffe or, Hockenhall, there is certainly not a back worth trying should need arise. The Club is worse off for players now than ever it was, even though it can put a good fifteen in the field when all are available. They are not always available, and it is no use bilking the fact that without them, or good substitutes, the chances of the team are absolutely nil.

Having got these few items off my chest, so to speak, I can deal more calmly with Saturday's encounter. To begin with it was played in a gale of wind which con- siderably upset the teams when they faced it. War- rington had its assistance in the first half and obtained a try instead of at least four from chances which went a begging. As a rule Warrington do not throw much away, but on Saturday they were generous when they could least afford it. Isherwood (2), Hockenhall (1), and the forwards (1) all muffed glorious openings, while to complete the chapter, in the second half Hallam made two mulls, which gave Leigh two tries. Added to all this they were beaten hopelessly in the scrimmage even before Boardman retired through his knee giving way. Naturally, therefore, Warrington lost the match.

When Leigh got the wind at their backs they soon showed that they were going to wipe off the odd try in less than no time. Molyneux commenced by dropping a goal, then Dunbavin scored a try, followed by Davies and Dunbavin converted. The same resourceful youth also dropped a further goal from centre and in the course of about a quarter of an hour Warrington were face to face with a score of 3 goals 2 tries. Leigh simply ran all over the Warrington defence and then eased off a bit while Warrington bucked up pluckily. There was no more scoring. but Warrington were soundly beaten, the score at the close just about representing the superiority of the Leigh team.

There was only one department of play where War- rington could claim any advantage and that was three- quarter. The home quartette was no match for the Warrington four either in speed or combination and this was the cause of the Warrington line only being crossed twice. Harris andDickenson played splendidly Fish and Isherwood also doing good work, though the latter was not at his best. Hallam was very unsteady, in fact.he was not like the same full back. Saturday must have been his "off-day." The Leigh forwards were splendid. They pushed the scrimmages and followed up in a style which will cause opposing teams some anxious moments They appeared to me to be the best all-round lot Warrington have met so far. The veteran Woodward is of decided use in the pack, and the others are all big fellows. Dunbavin was about the best back on the held. To a team like Warrington he would be worth a fortune for he can pass if it is necessary. Pearson was no better, though luckier, than Hallam, while Johnson was the best of the threequarters.

Needless to say the Leigh folk were hugely delighted at the success they gained and talked very loudly -of their prowess, but Warrington could beat them with a full side, and I hope to see them do it in the return engagement.

" Twinkle, twinkle, little star," etc.

llunslet escaped being starred last .week owing to their offence dating back to last season, but they have little to congratulate themselves upon for another charge still hangs over their heads. The second charge is one of signing on T Walsh after the player had signed for Birkenhead. Of that offence the club have been found guilty, and are to be reported to the General Committee for having committed a breach of Professional Law 11. If the higher court confirm the ruling, they will, no doubt, treat blunslet like the other guilty clubs.

Yorkshiremen obtained one crumb of comfort as the result of the week end matches, Bradford rather unex- pectedly defeating Salford, and thus obtaining the I second success achieved by a Yorkshire club against their rivals across the border. On the other band Run- corn and Oldham defeated Hunslet and Brighouse Rangers respectively very easily, and the record of matches between clubs in the respective counties now stands at fifteen victories for Lancashire ; two for Yorkshire.. The latter have thus a lot of lee way to make up. As was expected, Hull were just too strong for Halifax ; while an equally close match was seen at Swinton, where the colliery villagers were beaten by Broughton Rangers, who had only a point to spare. Oldham still lead, with five consecutive victories, Hull being the only Yorkshire club within measurable dis • tance of them.

The Swinton committee met on Tuesday evening and selected the following fifteen to represent them in the, match witlt Warrington :—A Chorley, back ; J T Lewis R J Messer, R Valentine, captain ; and Vernon Hemp. son, three quarters ; D Davies and A Williamson, half- backs ; J Preston, J Jones, J Evans, H .Barnett, C Pollitt, T Pomfret, E Vigors, and G Harris, forwards. Reserve, It Tickle. It will be seen that Morgan has been left off at half back, Williamson taking his place ; whilst Vigors takes Blackburn's place amongst the for- wards.

The Lancashire County trial game for the match with Cheshire on the-26th inat. will he played on Wedhesday afternoon at Wigan. J Fish has been chosen to play wing for the Probables, while Hallam (full back) and Fell, forward, will appear on the opposite side— Possibles. Dickenson is reserve centre three-quarter.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

Hope Hall Mission, Runcorn. , Efa,vest thanksgiving services were held on Sunday, when the special preacher was Mr W Arnold, of Liver- pool. The ladies of the congregation had worked indefatigably in the decoration of the hall, which had been tastefully accomplished with a variety of fruit, corn and flowers. At the afternoon service special hymns and solos were sung. There was a good attendance at the evening service, all the available room being occu— pied. Mr Arnold delivered an interesting address on the lessons to be derived from flowers. Special music was given, Mr Strange presiding at the organ. The services were continued on Monday evening, when an address was given by Mr J Spiby, of Widnes. The fruit was afterwards sold.

Mariners' Mission Church, Runcorn.

The harvest thanksgiving services in connection with this church commenced on Friday last, and were con. tinned on Sunday. The decorations had been carried out by the workers and friends interested in the place, and were extremely tasteful and picturesque in char- acter.--The preacher on Friday evening was Mr Joseph Stokes, licensed lay reader, while on Sunday tne pulpit was occupied in the afternoon by the vicar (Rev C R Ki!lick) and in the evening by Mr M Hall, of Birkenhead. The congregations were large, notwithstanding the unfavourable elements, and the offertories were in advance of last year.

The offertories amounted to £9 93 9d, which will be devoted to a fund for re-painting and decorating tbe interior of the church It is hoped that this may be accomplished before next March, when the Bishop of Liverpool has promised to hold a confirmation at Padgate.

- Bold-Street Wesleyan Church, Warrington. The harvest featival services were held in the above church on Sunday. The decorations were most effective and had been carried out by Mrs Biehop, Mrs Neville, Mrs Robinson, Mrs I Weaver, Mrs J Parkinson, ' and the Misses Weaver, Collin, Jones, Robinson (2), Barlow, Halt, and Wilson. The pulpit was beautifully adorned with corn, flowers, ferns, Sze, and the pillars were embellished with ivy and various kinds of flowers. The preacher at the morning service was the Rev W C J Neville, who occupied the pulpit at this place of worship for the first time since his appointment to the circuit, , and he delivered an interesting sermon from the words, " Verily, verily, I say unto you, unless a grain of wheat , fall into the ground and die, it remaineth alone. But if it die, it bringeth forth much fruit. He that inveth his life shall lose it, and he that hateth his life in this ' world, keepeth it unto life everlasting." The choir ' creditably rendered the anthem " Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem "(J H Maunder), in an able manner. In the evening, the Rev W C J Neville preached to a very large congregation from the words : " The harvest is the en,l of the world." The special anthem was Stainer's " Ye shall dwell in the lord," the solo being taken by Mr A Cotterill. Mrs It H Pearson also rendered the solo, "Entreat me not to leave thee" (Gouncd), in an effective manner. Mr F Hickman presided at the organ.

OR NEW SERIAL.

We have pleasure in announcing that we have secured for publication in our columns a Serial entitled...

"A UEE N'S SOLDIER," BY WALTER WOOD, AUTHOR OF " BARRACK AND BATTLEFIELD," " FAMOUS BRITISH WARSHIPS." " THROUGH BATTLE TO PROMIOTION," " A CAPTAIN IN THE FUSILIERS," "A SOLDIER OF THE LEGION," ETC.

===== 0002601_19011011_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
SOMETHING ABOUT THE AUTHOR.

EIR. WALTER WOOD first became well-known as a writer of stories dealing with military life through the medium of the London magazines.whose editors were not slow to recognise the unusually close acquaintance with barrack and campaign life and the dry humour which his work consistently re- vealed. He possesses the fundamental quality nsces- sary to success as a novelist—the ability to tell a story ; and the reader's interest never flags' Of his first published volume of military stories the London "Daily Telegraph " said, "There are few stronger or more captivating stories in the English language." A companion volume of naval articles won the rare honour, for a young writer, of a special—and generous—article in " The Saturday Review," from that distinguished naval critic, the late Admiral Ziolomb.

SOMETHING A.Bol/T THE STORY. " A QUERN'S SOLDIER "is a story of Yorkshire and of the South African battlefields. Its hero is a trooper in the Imperial Yeomanry, who leaves his father's farm and his sweetheart for the dusty veldt and the interminable hardships of the campaign. The author's experience in war subjects enables him. without apparent effort, to bring before the reader a brilliant series of pictures of life in the field. While he offers plenty of excitement in the way of skirmishing and of more serious battles, he never loses sight of the obligation as a novelist to give a human interest to his work, and the events of the war are shown, not as matters of dull history. but as they appeared to Mark Berm, trooper in the Imperial Yeornanry,while performing his duty as a soldier of the Queen. Bean is wounded in a skirmish, and is taken as a prisoner to Pretoria. Events enable him at length to escape, but only to undergo terrible sufferings. Perhaps this portion of the tale will be found to possess the most thrilliog interest for readers. Bennis experiences in his wild dash for liberty are partly founded on fact and they are graphically described. There are afterwards some Rill:apses of life in hospital, and the tale ends a! happily as everyone who makes the acquaintance Ot Mark Bean and his sweetheart wOul4 desire it tO do. "A Queen's Soldier," as the work of one wile knows haw to write intelligently about war, with- out overloading the picture, will, we confidently believe, be found to rank among the best stories which have been written about the South African strNgle.

Factory—Peokbara, S.E.—(ladvt. 4.) lanother excursion to the Leigh half and it looked for a minute as if Fish and Isherwood would score, but the opportunity was lost. Ganley made one or two good runs down the left wing, but after beating Harris he found Hockenhall a stumbling block. Play continued one sided to the close when Warrington retired beaten by 3 goals 2 tries (12 points) to 1 try (3 points). Runcorn v. Hunslet. There was but a poor crowd at Irwell•lane on Satur- day to watch the initial meeting of these clubs. Runcorn made one or two changes flunslet turning out as advertised. There was a strong wind blowing from end to end and the ground was a trifle slippery owing to one or two heavy showers just before the match. Th match was delayed at ls ast half an hour owing to thee visitors' baggage being delayed at Manchester. They were supplied with local white jerseys. The teams faced each other as follows : Runcorn.—Houghton, back ; Wilkinson, Warder, Butterworth and Moran, three quarters ; Jolley and Richardson, halves; Walker,Farmer, Lightfoot, Langley, Woods, Williams, Gayter and Dixon. Hunslet : Wright, back ; Hannah, W Goldthorpe Whitely and Horne, tbreequarters ; A. E Goldthorpe and T Gillings, halves ; Shooter,Wilson,Glew, Brooke, Lunn, Bageott, Walsh, Ramage and Wray, forwards. Runcorn lost the toss,Farmer starting against a strong breeze. The home contingent at once contested matters in the visitors' quar!ers where for a few minutes some exciting play was witnessed. From a Forum Richard- son secured, handing to Butterworth who dodged his opponents finely and scored a well merited try. Jolley failed at goal. Play settled at centre, Butterworth lo.ing conspicuous in stopping Horne as he was about to break away. Moran, at a critical moment saved. Shortly after Runcorn wero penalized A Goldthorpe taking the kick which went just outside. Hunslet continued to force the game but aided by the strenuous exertions of Jolley the hall was worked away. A combined movement by , Jolley, Walker and Wilkinson took the leather from end Ito end where Warder scored a splendid try. The goal kick again failed. The game became a trifle uninterest- ing, a poor kick by Wright giving .Runcorn the advan- tage. Hannah was thrown in touch when on the line but immediately after Goldthorpe dropped a fine goal. Runcorn continued to press and half time arrived with Runcorn leading by two tries to Hunslet a goal. At the restart, Runcorn, aided by the wind, attacked strongly. Hunslet were penalized, Houghton making an ineffectual shot at goal. Thu same player was also I conspicuous in stopping several dangerous rushes of the visitors. Butterworth with a splendid tricky run carried the ball on the line slipping when on the point of scor- ing. Scrummaging ensued, Runcorn combining splen- didly. Butterworth and Moran mach fine run, along touch, which, however, lacked finish. Several chances were missed owing to the home pack being too eager play settling in the visitors' quarters. Wright dallied with the ball too long and was grassed by Butterworth grandly on the line,Lightfoot touching the leather down as it slipped from the grasp of the visiting custodian. Jolley failed at goal. Hunslet could not stop the rush of the home forwards and two minutes later Moran crossed the line but an apparently fair try was disallowed. Hunslet were still kept busy on the defence. Wright saving at a critical moment and changing the venue to midfield. For obstruction the visitors were penalized Farmer failing at go tl. Houghton was again well to the front with several tricky runs, Butterworth gathering and handing to Walker who scored between the posts Farmer landing a goal. Runcorn continued to have the best of the game and romped over again the visitors being quite demoralized. Time arrived with Runcorn leading by 19 points to Hunslet two points. Broughton Rangers v. Swinton. Owing to the rivalry which exists between these clubs, and both of them being undef3sted in the league, much interest was centred in the encounter. People thronged the Swinton ground, and long before the time to commence there were over 10,000 present. Gradually the Rangers forced Swinton back when Chorley had his kick charged down, and the home line was reached. Here 9 James passed the ball to W James, who ran and passed to Hogg to score a try for the Rangers. Preston kicked a capital goal for Swinton. At half time the Rangers led by three points to two. In the second half both aides attacked in turn. Result :—Broughtou Rangers, one try (three points); Swinton, one goal (two points). Oldham v. Brighouse Rangers. At Oldham. Both clubs were well represented, but the weather was against a large attendance. The visi- tors pressed at the outset, and Thomas was compelled to touch down and kick dead twice in the first five minutes. The home team then brightened up, and a Prolonged attack ended in Huzzey scoring. Arthur Lees added another try very soon after, and before the interval Huzzey scored again after a pretty race along the touch line, and Sam Lees kicked A goal. Very shortly after the opening of the second half the Rangers secured their only points, Saville kicking a splendid goal from a penalty kick from almost the centre of the field. After this the defence of the visitors proved quite incap- able of contending with the dashes and the passing of

"WATTIE" DAVIES AND THE WARRINGTON FOOIBALL CLUB. A Refutation. The following letter has been issued to the press : Sir.—Our attention has been called to a letter from " Wattle " Davies which appeared in the " Yorkshire Evening Post "on Saturday the 14th September. We are instructed by theiCommittee of the Warrington Rugby Football Club to say that the statements therein contained are grossly misleading. It is quite untrue net Davies was struck by a specta ter whilst walking down the touch line. He was the aggressor and was so violent that be had to be removed by the police to the pavilion and afterwards to the dressing room. The summons which be states was taken out by him was only taken out after he had been served with a summons for assaulting a spectator named Brown, and was taken out by his solicitor simply as a matter of form. but Davies was neither struck on or off the field of play. It was at the earnest request of Davies and his solici- tor that the complainant and the committee withdrew the summons, upon Davies agreeing to apologise to the complainant and the committee, and be agreed to the apology being published in the Warrington papers and Paid all the costs of the prosecution. This is all the publicity the Warrington Committee have given to this unpleasant incident. Davies stated in his letter that " he was persuaded to come to terms to his sorrow." No pressure was brought to bear upon him to come to terms by ourselves or our clients as be was represented by his solicitor who advised him to settle the matter as stated in order to avoid a • conviction being recorded against him,thereby bringing the Batley Club into bad repute. The fact of the matter is,.that if he. had appeared before the magistrates the prosecution would have adduced ample evidence to have convicted him of a brutal and cowardly assault. The rest of Davies's letter is ridiculous and further comment is needless, Yours faithfully. (Signed) DAVIES AND FORSHAW. Sankey-street, Warrington. Burgess and wattle" Davies Sus- pended. : Meeting at Huddersfield Yesterday. At a meeting of the Northern League held at the George Hotel, Huddersfield on Friday afternoon Mr J. Clifford. Eluddersfield, presiding, the case of Davies, Batley, and Burgess, Warrington, came on for hearing. The referee and others gave evidence as to the alleged fighting, after which the committee decided to suspend Burgess for a month and Davies for 2 months.
